The eligibility to the centralised procedure was agreed upon by the EMA/CHMP on 12 December 2024.

'Sepalna is indicated as monotherapy for the maintenance treatment of adult patients with advanced epithelial (FIGO Stages III and IV) high-grade ovarian, fallopian tube or primary peritoneal cancer who are in response (complete or partial) following completion of first-line platinum-based chemotherapy.'

## 2. Introduction

## 2.1. Therapeutic context

Epithelial ovarian cancer (EOC) encompasses a heterogeneous group of malignant tumours originating from the epithelial cells of the ovary, fallopian tube, or peritoneum. Ovarian, fallopian tube, and primary peritoneal carcinomas are classified within a common FIGO staging system and are generally managed within the same clinical framework (Renz et al., Obstetrics and Gynecology, 2025; Ledermann et al .Ann Oncol 2024).

Ovarian cancer is the second most lethal gynaecologic malignancy worldwide and the leading cause of death from gynaecologic cancers in developed countries (González-Martín A et al., Ann Oncol 2023). According to GLOBOCAN 2022 estimates, approximately 324,000 new cases and 207,000 deaths from ovarian cancer occurred worldwide in 2022 (Bray et al. CA cancer J Clin, 2024) .

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

EOC is typically characterized by a progressive and often asymptomatic course. Because early disease is largely silent, more than two-thirds of women are diagnosed at an advanced FIGO stage (III-IV), when peritoneal dissemination and ascites are already present (Pérez-Fidalgo JA, et al . Clin Transl Oncol, 2024, Ledermann et al. Ann Oncol 2024). The disease tends to spread within the peritoneal cavity, while lymphatic and hematogenous dissemination also occur (Weidle et al., Cancer Genomics Proteomics, 2016, Barbolina et al., Cancers, 2018).

Symptoms are usually nonspecific, leading to diagnostic delays. Common features include abdominal bloating, distension, pelvic or abdominal pain, early satiety, urinary frequency, and fatigue (GonzálezMartín A, et al. Ann Oncol, 2023, Goff BA et al. Cancer 2007). In advanced disease, ascites, pleural effusions, weight loss, and bowel obstruction may occur (González-Martín A, et al. Ann Oncol 2023).

Risk factors include older age and postmenopausal status, nulliparity and infertility, endometriosis (Ali et al., Menopause Rev, 2023; Momenimovahed et al., Int J Womens Health 2019, Pearce et al., Lancet Oncol, 2012).

The cumulative risk of ovarian cancer to age 80 years has been estimated at 44% for BRCA1 and 17% for BRCA2 pathogenic variant carriers (Kuchenbaecker et al., JAMA 2017). Around 13-15% of ovarian cancers are attributable to heritable BRCA1/2 mutations, and additional cases carry somatic BRCA alterations (Konstantinopoulos PA et al. J Clin Oncol 2020).

Diagnostic work-up includes thorough history and physical examination, imaging (CT, MRI, or ultrasound), serum CA-125 measurement (elevated in ~85% of advanced cases but in only ~50% of stage I and not specific), genetic testing for germline and somatic BRCA mutations (González-Martín A, et al. Ann Oncol, 2023, Konstantinopoulos PA et al., J Clin Oncol, 2020).

EOC substantially impacts patients' daily functioning. Physical symptoms-fatigue, pain, digestive disturbances-and treatment side effects (neuropathy, surgical menopause, alopecia) impair quality of life (Ahmed-Lecheheb D, Joly F, J Cancer Surviv, 2016). Emotional distress, anxiety about recurrence, and social isolation are frequent. Even in remission, survivors face long-term sequelae and uncertainty regarding relapse, emphasizing the need for supportive and psychological care (Ahmed-Lecheheb D, Joly F, J Cancer Surviv, 2016, Joly F, et al., Ann Oncol, 2019, Osann K, et al. Gynecol Oncol, 2023, Roland et al. Psychooncology 2013)

First-line treatment of newly diagnosed ovarian cancer combines maximal cytoreductive surgery and platinum-based chemotherapy. The surgical target is R0 (no macroscopic residual disease). Patients unlikely to achieve R0 upfront may receive neoadjuvant chemotherapy followed by interval debulking surgery (González-Martín A, et al. Ann Oncol, 2023).

Standard first-line chemotherapy is carboplatin-paclitaxel q3w for 6 cycles. Bevacizumab can be added concurrently and continued as maintenance up to ~15 months total, particularly in high-risk stage III/IV (e.g., ascites/pleural effusion, sub-optimal cytoreduction).

Maintenance therapy after response to first-line (Stage III/IV) is recommended. Patients who are BRCA-mutated (germline or somatic) receive olaparib for 2 years or niraparib for 3 years. Bevacizumab can also be added to olaparib in this setting.

Patients who are BRCA-wild-type and HRD-positive receive olaparib + bevacizumab (if bevacizumab in induction) or niraparib monotherapy (PRIMA); rucaparib monotherapy (ATHENA-MONO) also improves PFS, with supportive 5-year updates.

Patients with a HRD-negative (HRP) status are recommended to receive bevacizumab maintenance (when started with chemo). PARP inhibitors yield modest and heterogeneous PFS gains without OS benefit to date (final PRIMA OS neutral).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.2. Aspects of development

The clinical development programme for senaparib in the maintenance treatment of adult patients with advanced epithelial high-grade ovarian, fallopian tube or primary peritoneal cancer who are in response following completion of first-line platinum-based chemotherapy includes:

Two Phase I studies:

- Study IMP4297-2016-CN01, a Phase I clinical study to evaluate the safety, tolerability, and pharmacokinetic characteristics of senaparib capsules in patients with advanced solid tumours refractory to standard treatments, conducted in China. This study serves as the basis for the choice of the intended dose, in which patients received doses ranging from 2 mg QD to 50 mg BID
- Study IMP4297-2016-AU01, an open-label, dose-escalation, Phase I study evaluating the safety and pharmacokinetics of senaparib in patients with advanced solid tumours, conducted in Australia.

One Phase II study

- IMP4297-2018-CN01 (study 201) a multicenter, open-label, single-arm, non-randomized Phase II clinical study conducted in China. It aims to evaluate the efficacy, safety, and tolerability of senaparib capsules in treating subjects with advanced ovarian cancer carrying germline and/or somatic BRCA1/2 mutations, and to observe the PK characteristics of senaparib. Patients received senaparib at the intended dose of 100 mg QD.

One Phase III study

- Study IMP4297-301 (study 301), is a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicenter Phase III clinical study conducted in China, aiming to evaluate the efficacy and safety of senaparib monotherapy maintenance treatment in subjects with FIGO stage III-IV ovarian cancer after first-line platinum-based chemotherapy.

Study 301 is the pivotal study in support of this MAA.

## 2.3. Description of the product

Senaparib is a poly (ADP-ribose) polymerase (PARP) inhibitor, with inhibitory effect on PARP-1 and PARP-2 enzyme activities. Cytotoxicity induced by senaparib has been observed in tumour cell lines both with and without BReast CAncer (BRCA) 1/2 tumour suppressor genes deficiencies.

The claimed therapeutic indication was as follows:

Sepalna is indicated as monotherapy for the maintenance treatment of adult patients with advanced epithelial (FIGO Stages III and IV) high-grade ovarian, fallopian tube or primary peritoneal cancer who are in response (complete or partial) following completion of first-line platinum-based chemotherapy.

The final agreed therapeutic indication is:

Sepalna is indicated as monotherapy for the maintenance treatment of adult patients with advanced (FIGO Stages III and IV) epithelial high-grade ovarian, fallopian tube or primary peritoneal cancer who are in response (complete or partial) following completion of first-line platinum-based chemotherapy.

Posology:

The recommended dose is 100 mg senaparib (five 20 mg capsules), once daily.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Patients should start the maintenance treatment with Sepalna no later than 8 weeks after completion of their final dose of the platinum containing regimen.

## Duration of treatment

Patients can continue treatment until radiological disease progression, unacceptable toxicity or completion of 2 years treatment.

## Missed dose

If the patient misses a dose by more than 4 hours of the time it is usually taken, the patient should not take the missed dose and should take the next dose at the regularly scheduled time.

## Dose adjustments for adverse reactions

The recommended dose modifications for adverse reactions are listed in Table 3 and Table 4.

The starting dose can be adjusted one step at a time, up to three times.

Table 3 Dose modification steps

| Starting dose         | 100 mg / day (five 20 mg capsules)   |
|-----------------------|--------------------------------------|
| First dose reduction  | 80 mg / day (four 20 mg capsules)    |
| Second dose reduction | 60 mg / day (three 20 mg capsules)   |
| Third dose reduction  | 40 mg / day* (two 20 mg capsules)    |

*If further dose reduction below 40 mg/day is required, Sepalna should be discontinued. Further dose reduction may be considered if Sepalna needs to be concomitantly administered with strong CYP3A4. Sepalna dose can be reduced down to 20 mg/day.

CTCAE: Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events.

## Method of administration

Sepalna is for oral use. The capsules should be swallowed whole with water. They should not be chewed or crushed.

They should be taken at least one hour before or two hours after the ingestion of food.

This medicinal product has been approved for marketing in China by China's National Medical Products Administration (NMPA) on 14 January 2025 for the following indication: senaparib for the maintenance treatment of adult patients with advanced epithelial (FIGO stages III and IV) high-grade ovarian, fallopian tube, or primary peritoneal cancer who are in response (complete or partial) following completion of first-line platinum-based chemotherapy.

## 2.4. Inspection issues

## 2.4.1. Good manufacturing practice (GMP) inspection(s)

The two China-based manufacturing sites responsible for the manufacture of the intermediate product and for the finished product have undergone pre-approval inspections by European Medicines Agency (EMA) inspectors in January 2026. The GMP certificates were subsequently provided as part of this procedure.

All other manufacturing sites hold valid GMP certificates from inspections conducted by EEA competent authorities, or by competent authorities of countries where a Mutual Recognition Agreement (MRA) is in operation.

## 2.4.2. Good laboratory practice (GLP) inspection(s)

Fourteen non-clinical safety GLP studies have been submitted, completed between November 2015 and April 2023, conducted in China, in WuXi AppTec, except some specific phases of expertise in the USA. Test facilities which have carried out the GLP studies belong or were part of a compliant monitoring program in Belgium. All pivotal toxicology studies complied with OECD GLP regulations. The CHMP did not identify a need for a GLP inspection.

## 2.4.3. Good clinical practice (GCP) inspection(s)

The Applicant declares that the clinical study report submitted is compliant with the guidance ICH Topic E 3 Structure and Content of Clinical Study Reports as well as the Note for guidance on the inclusion of appendices to clinical study reports in marketing authorisation applications.

Study IMP4297-301 was conducted in compliance with internationally uniform ethics principles, including the Declaration of Helsinki, guiding principles of International Council for Harmonisation (ICH), Good Clinical Practice (GCP), and other applicable local regulations and policies, including the archiving of essential documents. No breaches in GCP compliance for study IMP4297-301 were identified.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A request for routine GCP inspection has been adopted by the CHMP in September 2025 for the following clinical study: IMP4297-301. Overall, the findings are not of nature to challenge the efficacy outcomes.

## 3. Quality aspects

## 3.1. Introduction

The finished product is presented as a hard capsule containing 20 mg of senaparib as active substance.

Other ingredients are: hypromellose phthalate, microcrystalline cellulose, mannitol, crospovidone, colloidal silicon dioxide and magnesium stearate for the capsule content; and gelatin and titanium dioxide for the capsule shell.

The product is available in 150 mL high density polyethylene (HDPE) white bottles with an aluminium heat-induction seal and polypropylene (PP) child resistant closures. Each bottle contains 75 capsules.

## 3.2. Active substance

## 3.2.1. General information

The chemical name of senaparib is 5-fluoro-1-(4-fluoro-3-(4-(pyrimidin-2-yl) piperazine-1-carbonyl) benzyl) quinazoline-2, 4(1H, 3H)-dione dehydrate corresponding to the molecular formula

C24H20F2N6O3·2H2O. It has a relative molecular mass 514.48 and the following structure:

Figure 1: Active substance structure

<!-- image -->

The active substance is a non-hygroscopic crystalline white to off-white powder or granules practically insoluble in water, freely soluble in dimethyl sulfoxide, slightly soluble in tetrahydrofuran and acetonitrile, very slightly soluble in methanol, and practically insoluble in buffer solutions of different pH conditions (pH 1.2, pH 4.5, pH 6.8, pH 7.4). senaparib is classified as a BCS Class IV compound.

Senaparib has a non - chiral molecular structure.

Polymorphism has been observed for senaparib. The dominant hydrate crystal form was selected for development. Polymorphism is controlled in the active substance specification.

## 3.2.2. Manufacture, process controls and process development

Description of manufacturing process and process controls Satisfactory evidence of GMP compliance has been presented.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Senaparib is synthesised in five main steps using well defined starting materials with acceptable specifications.

## Control of materials

Extensive fate and purge studies on potential and actual impurities of the proposed starting materials have been carried out. It has been shown that no impurities arising from the starting materials impact the impurity profile of the final active substance with the proposed specification and process control strategy

One of the originally proposed starting materials was not accepted by CHMP and a major objection (MO1) was raised as this compound is introduced only one chemical transformation away from the final active substance and not enough steps would be conducted under GMP.

This proximity raised concerns about the potential for contamination or impurities that might not be adequately addressed by the existing manufacturing process.

Although the applicant claimed that that starting material was of commercial origin, this claim was not substantiated in line with ICH Q11. Specifically, it was not demonstrated that the starting materialis a commodity chemical from a pre-existing non-pharmaceutical market, which is a key criterion for considering a starting material as being of commercial origin.

To address the MO, the applicant agreed to redefine to earlier synthetic precursors. The redefined starting materials are of commercial origin in line with ICH Q11 definition and sufficient information was provided in regarding their impurity profile and controls. Former proposed starting material is redefined as an isolated intermediate with unchanged specifications. The process was updated to include Step 5. Overall, the control strategy of the updated dossier is acceptable.

## Control of critical steps and intermediates

Two Critical Process Parameters (CPPs) have been identified.

The control strategy for intermediates is supported by experimental data (spike and purge) for impurities.

The description of in-process controls has been complemented and is sufficient.

## Process validation

Information concerning the validation of the active manufacturing process has not been presented; this approach is acceptable for a non-sterile active substance.

## Manufacturing process development

The commercial manufacturing process for the active substance was developed in parallel with the clinical development program. The manufacturing process of senaparib active substance underwent three stages of development, with the same synthetic route but improvements in each stage to better control impurities and polymorphic form. Stage 1 was for early clinical trials, stage 2 optimized the process for pivotal clinical trials, and stage 3 focused on process validation for commercial supply. Key optimisations included changing the bromination reaction method, improving yield and purity, and ensuring the active substance was in the dihydrate form with the target crystal form. Changes introduced have been presented in sufficient detail and have been justified.

Process characterisation studies were performed taking into account critical quality attributes (CQAs) of the active substance. The polymorphic form was claimed as a CQA despite the active substance being completely dissolved in the finished product manufacturing process.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The development studies were primarily one variable at a time (OFAT) and worst-case experiments, with no multivariate studies or analysis conducted. Studies are sufficient as confirmation of process robustness but not to support process flexibility. Only NORs are included in the commercial process description, this is acceptable.

## 3.2.3. Characterisation and impurities

The chemical structure of active substance was adequately elucidated by a combination of highresolution mass spectrometry (HRMS), infrared (IR) absorption spectroscopy, ultraviolet (UV) absorption spectroscopy, nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR) spectroscopy ( 1 H, 13 C, 19 F) and elemental analysis (EA). The dihydrate form was characterised by X-ray powder diffraction (XRPD), differential scanning calorimetry (DSC), dynamic vapor sorption (DVS) and microcrystal electron diffraction (Micro ED).

The characterisation of the active substance and its impurities are in accordance with the EU guideline on chemistry of active substances. Potential and actual impurities are well discussed with regards to their origin and characterised. The discussion about process organic impurities is thorough and complete; potential impurities are controlled by the starting materials' and intermediates' specifications; actual impurities have been studied across the process in terms of fate &amp; purge.

Despite the ICH S9 indication, the applicant has elected to fully comply with ICH M7. Potentially mutagenic impurities are controlled either by Option 2 (control in intermediates) or Option 4 (process controls).

Residual solvents have been controlled in line with ICH Q3C except for N,N-diisopropylethylamine (DIPEA) which is not included in ICH Q3C and is controlled using other toxicologically validated limit.

## 3.2.4. Control of active substance

The active substance release specification includes tests for description (visual), identification (IR, HPLC), polymorpic form (XRPD), related substances (HPLC), assay (HPLC), residual solvents (GC), residue on ignition (Ph. Eur.) and water content (KF).

The testing parameters included in the specification are broadly in line with ICH Q6A.

Impurities limits are in accordance with ICH Q3A qualification threshold (0.15%) for specified impurities and identification threshold (0.10%) for unspecified impurities hence are acceptable.

Adequate justifications to support the omission of tests for specific residual solvent, elemental impurities, other inorganic impurities, particle size distribution (PSD), potential mutagenic impurities and microbial limits have been provided.

## Analytical procedures and reference standards

Overall, the analytical methods have been sufficiently described and (non-compendial methods) appropriately validated in accordance with the ICH guidelines. Satisfactory information regarding the reference standards used for assay and impurities testing has been presented.

The methods are adequate to control the quality of the active substance on a routine basis; the stability indicative nature of the methods has been proven by carrying out forced degradation studies.

## Batch analysis

Batch analysis data from 9 production scale batches representative of the commercial process have been presented. The results are generally consistent from batch to batch and the data indicates that the process is under control. Levels of impurities are consistently very low and remain below 0.10% in all cases. The results are within the specifications and confirm consistency of the manufacturing process.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3.2.5. Stability

The retest period of 48 months when stored up to 30°C in the proposed container is justified by the available stability data.

Stability data from three production scale batches of active substance from the proposed manufacturer stored in the intended commercial package for up to 48 months under long term (25 ºC / 60% RH) and intermediate conditions (30 ºC / 65% RH) and for up to 6 months under accelerated conditions (40 ºC / 75% RH) according to the ICH guidelines were provided.

The following parameters were tested: appearance, polymorphic form, related substances, assay, water content and microbial limits. The analytical procedures used were the same as for release, with the addition of the test for microbial limits, and were stability indicating as demonstrated through studies under stressed conditions.

No significant changes were observed in appearance, assay, water content, polymorphic form, related substances, microbial limits test. All results complied with specification. Results under stress conditions (40 ºC, 60 ºC, 25 ºC /92.5%RH, light) were also provided. The results showed that senaparib is not sensitive to high humidity, slightly sensitive to light and more sensitive to high temperature.

Photostability testing following the ICH guideline Q1B was performed on one batch and showed formation of one photo degradation impurity; the CCS ensures protection from light (external aluminium foil).

A retest period of 48 months is proposed for senaparib active substance when stored up to 30°C.

The commercial active substance is packaged in double low-density polyethylene (LDPE) bags, which complies with Commission Regulation (EU) 10/2011, as amended, and secured with cable tie. Then the bag is placed inside aluminium foil bag and heat-sealed. Finally, the closed bag is placed into a fibre drum with iron cap for storage and shipping.

The stability indicating nature of the methods was confirmed through studies under stressed conditions.

## 3.3. Finished medicinal product

## 3.3.1. Description of the product and pharmaceutical development

## Description of the product

The finished product is an immediate release formulation for oral administration presented as a hard capsule containing 20 mg of senaparib.

Senaparib capsules are packaged in a HDPE bottle with an aluminium heat-induction seal and PP child resistant closure.

Each senaparib capsule contains the senaparib, hypromellose phthalate as amorphous solid dispersion (ASD) carrier, microcrystalline cellulose and mannitol as diluents, crospovidone as disintegrant, colloidal silicon dioxide as glidant, and magnesium stearate as lubricant.

The composition of the finished product is adequately described. The grade and function of the excipients are specified.

The amount of active substance in the capsule is approximately 5% w/w, and it represents 25% w/w of the intermediate amorphous solid dispersion (ASD). Therefore, the active substance is not considered low-dosed.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Pharmaceutical development

The formulation development of senaparib capsules has been adequately described.

The physicochemical properties of the active substance have been adequately discussed. Senaparib is practically insoluble in aqueous media at different pH values and has poor absorption. The active substance is classified as BCS IV. To enhance its oral bioavailability, senaparib was formulated into an amorphous solid dispersion (ASD) obtained by spray-drying. Later optimisation of the capsule formulation consisted in improving flowability for capsule filling by adding external excipients to the ASD.

The quality target product profile (QTPP) and critical quality attributes (CQAs) of senaparib ASD and senaparib capsules were determined based on the patient's needs and the intended finished product performance.

All the excipients used in the senaparib capsules are commonly used for oral solid dosage forms. The drug-excipient compatibility data provide a rational basis for selecting excipients.

The ASD being a critical component of the formulation, the selection of the solid dispersion carrier hypromellose phthalate (HPMCP) with a ratio of 3:1 to the active substance, and the solvent system methanol: tetrahydrofuran (3:7), were thoroughly investigated and justified.

Despite the efforts to optimise the active substance loading in the formulation, a capsule size 0el dosed at 20 mg of senaparib is proposed for commercialisation. The posology recommends taking five capsules (100 mg) once daily. Even though the medicinal product is indicated for adults only, considering the size of the capsules, CHMP raised the concern that certain patients may have difficulty for swallowing these capsules and asked the applicant to discuss whether acceptability of the treatment had been assessed as part of the clinical studies, justify why the capsules should be swallowed whole and cannot be chewed or crushed and discuss any possible alternative strategy for those bot able to swallow the capsule. According to the Applicant's response, no feedback regarding swallowing difficulties was received neither from patients during the clinical trial period, nor from the use of the product on the Chinese market for over a year. Impact of chewing, crushing, or opening the capsules and mixing the content with water or apple sauce, versus swallowing the capsules whole, has been investigated. Results show that in vitro dissolution is accelerated when capsules are manipulated as described. Based on the modified dissolution profiles, adherence to the proposed administration mode and SmPC instructions is accepted considering the absence of complaints by the patients so far, the indication, and acknowledging that the compliance issue may only impact some adult patients.

The development of the quality control (QC) dissolution method is justified, with a suitable medium and paddle agitation speed selected. The discriminatory power of the dissolution method versus larger ASD particle sizes and, to certain extent, crystalline impurities, is demonstrated. Dissolution profiles between pivotal batches, bioequivalence batches and process validation batches were found similar. Of note, the dissolution test conditions have been changed in the course of the pharmaceutical development.

The risk of polymorphic transformation during the production process and upon storage of the ASD and of the capsules, has been addressed. It was concluded to be low under the controlled process parameters. A XRPD routine test to confirm the amorphous state of the product and a limit test method with better specificity and sensitivity to check for impurity crystalline forms, were developed and implemented throughout development to detect potential crystalline forms of Senaparib in the ASD and in the capsules. Data provided throughout the dossier, using the XRPD limit test, demonstrated the absence of polymorphic transformations of the ASD throughout storage and transport of the ASD

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

(long-term and accelerated data in support of 24-months bulk holding time), following the manufacture, storage and in-use of the capsules (long-term and accelerated stability data on three primary batches, in-use stability data). In routine, it is proposed not to carry out the tests on the capsules, but only on the ASD, which is agreed.

The formulation and process of senaparib capsules were initially developed based on a 10 mg strength. One manufacturer site initially responsible for both senaparib ASD production and capsule production. The produced 10 mg capsules have been used in phase I dose escalation clinical trials in China and Australia, as well as in phase II and phase III clinical trials in China. Then, senaparib ASD production has been moved to a commercial site, and senaparib capsule production has been moved to another commercial site (referred to as 10 mg commercial formulation). The formulation, process steps and equipment principles of the 10 mg capsule remained unchanged between the different sites. The produced capsules have been used in phase III trials in China. A bioequivalence study was conducted to compare the 10mg capsules produced before and after manufacturing sites change (IMP4297-108), showing bioequivalence under fasting conditions.

To improve patient compliance, a capsule formulation containing 20 mg of senaparib was further developed. This final formulation contains the same intermediate ASD (unchanged). Only the external excipient ratios were changed. A bioequivalence study between 10 mg and 20 mg capsules manufactured at the development site was performed (IMP4297-107). The results demonstrated that the 10 mg and 20 mg capsules manufactured at the previous site were bioequivalent. Further to site transfer, other bioequivalence studies were conducted to compare the 10 mg and 20 mg capsules (referred to as 20 mg commercial formulation) produced at the commercial site (IMP4297-110-1, IMP4297-110-2, and IMP4297-113). The results demonstrated that the 10 mg and 20 mg capsules manufactured at the commercial site were bioequivalent under fasting conditions.

All excipients are well known pharmaceutical ingredients and their quality is compliant with Ph. Eur. Standards. Following a request from the CHMP, the applicant has added the functionality-related characteristic control of particle size distribution (PSD) to the specification for mannitol 200SD and MCC PH200, and the functionality-related characteristic control of specific surface area to the specification for Aerosil 200. Appropriate water content limits have also been added to the specifications for all excipients.

For solvent tetrahydrofuran (used to obtain the ASD intermediate of the finished product), in-house specifications were established since no Ph. Eur. monograph is published. The specification was completed with limits for individual &amp; total impurities. Adequately justified acceptance criteria were proposed.

## Manufacturing process development

The choice of the manufacturing process has been driven by the physico-chemical properties of the active substance, i.e. its very poor solubility, and the preference to have a conventional oral solid dosage form available for the treatment. The choice of manufacturing a solid dispersion by spraydrying, and filling the final blend into hard capsules, is considered justified.

Principles of Quality by Design were followed during process development, in particular the use of iterative risk assessments (RAs) and the use of design of experiments (DoEs).

Outcomes of the RAs, with rationale, are presented throughout this section in tabular format to justify the studies conducted. The DoEs performed were not designed to capture multivariate interactions and establish design spaces, but to optimise the relevant process parameters. The acceptable ranges predefined at pilot scale were confirmed at commercial scale. These studies support the process as set in the process description.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

During spray-drying development, process development studies showed that the most impacted CQAs were the ASD PSD, the ASD residual solvents, and the capsule dissolution. Process parameters were optimised to meet the targeted CQAs.

Development of the final blending and lubrication steps and capsule filling step was straightforward. The proposed parameters' settings in the process description are supported by the process development studies.

## 3.3.2. Manufacture of the product and process controls

At the time of the MAA submission, these sites did not hold valid GMP certificates for the claimed manufacturing activities (MO2), but have undergone pre-approval inspections by European Medicines Agency (EMA) inspectors in early 2026 . The outcome of these inspections and the GMP certificates have been made available by D181 of the MAA procedure.

The manufacturing process consists of the following main steps: spray-drying, sieving and packaging of the intermediate ASD; blending, lubrication and encapsulation.

THF is used to obtain the ASD intermediate of the finished product and is removed following spraydrying and secondary drying to ICHQ3C compliant levels in the final finished product. CHMP acknowledged that the purity criterion (≥99.8%) that is applied for this non-compendial solvent is very tight, but, nonetheless, requested the applicant to ensure that all known and unknown impurities of THF, besides peroxides, BHT and water also e.g. dihydrofuran, 4-hydroxybutanal and aldehydes such as methacrolein, etc. are sufficiently controlled. Additional limits for specified and any unspecified impurity(ies) and associated validated analytical procedure(s) are required, unless otherwise justified (MO3). In his response the applicant addressed potential impurities with a boiling point lower than or close to that of THF, potential THF impurities with a boiling point (significantly) higher than that of THF and introduced proposes to further strengthen the quality control of the THF raw material by introducing a limit for individual (unspecified) impurity (≤0.1%) and total impurities (≤0.2%). This is acceptable.

A detailed process description is provided for the manufacture of the finished product intermediate ASD and the finished product. Narrative description includes equipment type, equipment capacity, and relevant process parameters (targets and mainly NORs). The proposed ranges are supported by the process development studies.

Critical steps are identified, and critical process parameters are defined.

In-process controls are listed with satisfactory sampling and acceptance criteria.

Specifications for the intermediate ASD are set and considered justified. Residual solvents, impurities, senaparib amorphous state and ASD particle size distribution are adequately controlled with history of batch results showing compliance with pre-defined limits. Corresponding analytical procedures are described and validated. The control strategy for the intermediate product ASD is overall satisfactory.

The packaging of the intermediate ASD is described (LDPE bags inside Aluminium foil bags and HDPE drums). A shelf-life of 24 months is claimed for the ASD when stored ≤25°C and supported by appropriate stability data.

Process validation has been conducted on three full-scale batches of the intermediate and finished product (400,000 capsules), demonstrating robustness and consistency in meeting specifications. It has been demonstrated that the manufacturing process is capable of producing the finished product of intended quality in a reproducible manner. The in-process controls are adequate for this type of manufacturing process and pharmaceutical form.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3.3.3. Control of Finished Product

## Specifications

The finished product release and shelf-life specifications include appropriate tests for the proposed dosage form. These include: appearance (visual), identification (HPLC, UV), related substances (HPLC), content uniformity (HPLC), dissolution (HPLC), water content (KF), residual solvent (GC), microbial limits (Ph. Eur. ) and assay (HPLC).

The proposed analytical procedures and corresponding specification limits are adequate for routine control of the finished product at release and at shelf-life. They are in accordance with ICH guidelines, Ph. Eur. and batch data at release and at shelf-life. The limits for related impurities are in line with ICH Q3B. The limit for residual solvent is in line with ICH Q3C.

Justification is provided for not including the XRPD tests, specific degradation impurity and elemental impurities in the finished product specifications, which is acceptable.

The applicant has discussed the potential polymorphic transformation risk and developed an XRPD limit test method which was used to collect the release and stability study data across multiple batches of senaparib ASD and capsules. The test results indicate that no polymorphic transformation occurred in Senaparib ASD and capsules under the specified packaging and storage conditions. This, in combination with the understanding of the manufacturing process, justifies that the risk of polymorphic transformation during the manufacturing, storage and use of Senaparib ASD to capsules is very low. The crystalline form test item is not included in the specification of senaparib capsules. However, to strictly control the product quality of subsequent commercial production, crystalline form (XRPD routine test and XRPD limit test) is included in the specification of senaparib ASD.

The potential presence of elemental impurities in the finished product was assessed following a riskbased approach in line with the ICH Q3D Guideline for Elemental Impurities. Batch analysis data from six batches using a validated ICP-MS method was provided, demonstrating that each relevant elemental impurity was not detected above 30% of the respective PDE. Based on the risk assessment and the presented batch data it can be concluded that it is not necessary to include any elemental impurity controls. The information on the control of elemental impurities is satisfactory.

A risk assessment concerning the potential presence of nitrosamine impurities in the ASD and the finished product have been provided: the absence of risk despite the presence of secondary amines and the presence of nitrites in extragranular excipients was confirmed by testing on the finished product (relevant nitrosamine not detected). Based on the information provided, there is no risk of nitrosamine impurities in the finished product. Therefore, no specific control measures are deemed necessary.

## Analytical procedures and reference standards

The analytical procedures used have been adequately described. Analytical procedures are also described for methods used during development but not part of routine control strategy.

Non-compendial methods have been appropriately validated in accordance with the ICH Q2. Compendial methods have been sufficiently verified and shown suitable for the intended use.

The analytical method for assay and related substances has been demonstrated to be stability indicating.

Detailed information regarding the reference standards used for assay and impurities testing are presented.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Batch analysis

Full-scale batch analysis data (three PPQ batches and four CPV batches) of the finished product are provided. The results are within the specifications and confirm consistency of the manufacturing process.

## 3.3.4. Stability of the product

## Container closure system

The container closure system is HDPE bottle with an aluminium heat-induction seal and PP child resistant closure. It is considered appropriate for intended storage. Its specifications are acceptable and are in compliance with appropriate standards such as the Ph. Eur. 3.1.5 and 3.1.6. Compliance with ISO 8317 standard for child-resistant closures has also been confirmed.

## Stability data

ICH stability studies have been conducted on three primary batches (PPQ batches) placed on stability under accelerated conditions (40 °C/75% RH) for 6 months and intermediate and long-term conditions (30 °C/65% RH and 25 °C/60% RH) for up to 36 months. The container used in the stability studies is the one proposed for routine storage.

The following parameters were tested: appearance assay, related substances, water content, dissolution microbial limits, amorphous state by XRPD and phtalic acid. The dissolution method changed in the course of development; the final QC method has been used from the 18-months timepoint.

Considering that the dissolution data in the accelerated study were obtained with the development method, new accelerated stability study using the final QC dissolution method on 3 commercial-scale batches has been conducted. Upon request, the full accelerated stability data generated using the final QC-dissolution method has been provided in Module 3.2.P.8.3

All the results under long-term, intermediate and accelerated conditions are compliant with specifications. The XRPD limit test was used to monitor potential Senaparib polymorphic transformation risk. The test results indicated that no polymorphic transformation occurred in senaparib capsules under any of the conditions tested. Stability studies demonstrated the active substance is stable in the amorphous state. The storage condition has been revised during the procedure. Based on the dataset submitted, the proposed shelf-life for the finished product of 36 months without any special storage conditions, is approvable.

Considering the claimed storage period of 24 months for the ASD intermediate product, clarification and a statement have been provided that the finished product shelf-life starts from the introduction of the active substance into the spray-dried solution.

Photostability testing following the ICH guideline Q1B was performed on one batch. Results indicate that the finished product is not sensitive to light conditions tested.

In-use stability has been conducted in worst-case conditions (40°C±2°C/75%RH±5%RH) for 20 days on one batch near its expiration date. The actual use of a bottle (75 capsules) is 15 days according to the recommended posology (5 capsules/day), up to 25 days in case of dose reduction. Considering that no significant changes have been observed neither in the ICH stability studies nor in the in-use study, no additional in-use batch testing is requested, and an in-use shelf-life is not claimed in the SmPC/package leaflet.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3.3.5. Post approval change management protocol(s)

N/A

## 3.3.6. Adventitious agents

All materials used for the production of Sepalna (senaparib capsules), including the gelatin utilised in the empty hard capsules, comply with European Community Directive 2001/83/EC and its amendment 2003/63/EC.

None of the excipients, except the gelatine, are from animal origin as indicated in the manufacturers' statement. The BSE/TSE free statements are provided for all excipients. CEPs for concerned gelatine sources are presented.

## 3.4. Discussion and conclusions on quality aspects

The finished product is presented as a hard capsule containing 20 mg of senaparib as active substance. The active substance is classified as BCS IV. To enhance its oral bioavailability, senaparib was formulated into an amorphous solid dispersion (ASD) obtained by spray-drying.

Three quality MOs were raised during the procedure and satisfactorily addressed by the applicant. These pertained to one of the proposed active substance starting material (MO1), the lack of GMP certificates for the manufacturers of the ASD finished product intermediate and the finished product (MO2) and the control of THF used to manufacture the ASD (MO3).

Information on development, manufacture and control of the active substance and finished product have been presented in a satisfactory manner. The results of tests carried out indicate satisfactory consistency and uniformity of important product quality characteristics, and these in turn lead to the conclusion that the product should have a satisfactory and uniform performance in the clinic.

## 3.4.1. Recommendations for future quality development

Not applicable.

## 4. Non-clinical aspects

## 4.1. Introduction

The non-clinical development programme for senaparib comprised pharmacology, pharmacokinetic and toxicology studies conducted in accordance with ICH S9 to support its development as an oral PARP inhibitor. The programme included in vitro and in vivo pharmacodynamic studies, ADME investigations, safety pharmacology and repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats and dogs.

## 4.2. Analytical methods

LC-MS/MS method was qualified for the quantitatively analyses of senaparib in several nonclinical matrices in PK and TK studies. The analytical methods used in studies conducted by the applicant are considered acceptable. However, no validation of analytical methods used for determination of metabolite M9 are reported.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.3. Pharmacology

## 4.3.1. Pharmacodynamics

## 4.3.1.1. Primary pharmacodynamics

## In vitro

Senaparib exhibits inhibitory activity against both PARP1 and PARP2, both implicated in DNA damage repair. The proposed indication is the following: maintenance treatment of adult patients with advanced epithelial (FIGO Stages III and IV) high-grade ovarian, fallopian tube or primary peritoneal cancer who are in response (complete or partial) following completion of first-line platinum-based chemotherapy.

Specificity of inhibition:

The inhibitory effect of senaparib was evaluated against PARP1, PARP2, PARP3, PARP5A, PARP5B, PARP6, PARP7, PARP10, PARP11, PARP12, PARP14 and PARP15 enzymes with IC50 values of 0.266 nM, 0.114 nM, 17.084 nM, 9.67 nM, 16.045 nM, 43.76 nM, 23.87 nM, 704.42 nM, 239.52, 250.5 nM, 1671,73 and 150.07 nM. Senaparib is a demonstrated inhibitor of both PARP1 and PARP2. The unbound Cmax at the intended clinical dose is 275 nM. Therefore, at the clinical exposure, senaparib is not expected to be selective for PARP1 and PARP2, and inhibition is also anticipated on PARP5A/B, PARP6, PARP7, PARP10, PARP11, PARP12 and PARP15.

Olaparib was used as comparator in these inhibition assays, and its activity against PARP1 and PARP2 appeared to be similar. However, senaparib appeared to inhibit PARP6 enzyme at a clinically relevant concentrations whereas olaparib demonstrated to be more selective to PARP1/2. Senaparib appeared to inhibit PARP5a enzyme in the in vitro enzyme activity assay and exhibited inhibitory activity comparable to that of XAV939, a reference compound for PARP5a inhibition.

In a separate study, senaparib showed slightly greater PARP1/PARP2 inhibition potency than niraparib and rucaparib.

No data was presented on PARPs from rat or dog. However, due to the conserved nature of PARP proteins across species and, most importantly, the occurrence of toxicity at exposure levels below those anticipated clinically, the findings observed in rat and dog are considered relevant to the pharmacological activity of senaparib in humans.

Mechanism of action:

Several BRCA or PTEN-mutant tumour cell lines, and WT tumour cell lines, including HRP and HRD cell lines and ovarian cancer cell lines were studied in vitro. Cytotoxicity, apoptosis and trapping are the three endpoints that were measured for PARP inhibition. Cytotoxicity assays showed that treatment with senaparib inhibited cell proliferation in both HRD and HRP cell lines, with slightly more potency toward BRCA-mutant (HRD) than BRCAwt HRP cell lines. Senaparib showed more cytotoxic potency than niraparib and rucaparib in all tested cell lines. Senaparib IC50 determined in these cytotoxicity studies were in the same range than unbound Cmax at the intended clinical dose of 100 mg.

The Applicant investigated PARP trapping potency in a comparative study with olaparib using a likelyHRD prostate cancer cell line, and with niraparib and rucaparib in an BRCAwt HRP ovarian cancer cell line and an BRCA-mutant HRD breast cancer cell line. Senaparib PARP trapping activity was more potent than olaparib in the prostate cancer cell line and more potent than niraparib and rucaparib in both cell lines tested.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Only one study investigated apoptosis and demonstrated senaparib apoptosis in cell line with BRCA1 mutation.

## In vivo

The in vivo proof-of-concept was determined by using xenograft mice model with MDA-MB-436 human breast cancer carrying BRCA1 mutation and in patient-derived xenograft mice model with BR-05-0028 human breast cancer carrying BRCA1 mutation. Senaparib was injected subcutaneously in MDA-MB-436 xenograft model in BALB/c nude mice at 2.5, 5, 10 mg/kg 5 days per week for 5 weeks or one a week for 3 weeks and 20, 40 and 80 mg/kg once daily up to 3 weeks. Senaparib showed a statistically significant tumour inhibition effect (measured by decrease of the tumour volume) in MDA-MB-436 human breast cancer nude mice model from the lowest dose 5 mg/kg, with a comparable activity than olaparib at 100 mg/kg, this was confirmed when administrated once weekly. Senaparib presented a significant dose response. The cure rate was non-existent at 2.5 mg/kg and 5 mg/kg and limited at 10 mg/kg (20% maximum) and more pronounced at 20 mg/kg (up to 30%), 40 mg/kg (up to 80% but not persistent 40% at the end of the experiment) and at 80 mg/kg (up to 80-90%) at D84 after 28 days. An additional study was conducted with this cell line as xenograft to compare senaparib, niraparib, and rucaparib in the same study. Senaparib achieved similar tumour regression with a much lower dose than niraparib and rucaparib.

In the BR-05-0028 human breast patient-derived xenograft mice model, senaparib was administrated at 5, 10 and 20 mg/kg once daily for 28 days and olaparib was used as comparator at 100 mg/kg. All tested dose levels caused a statistically significant antitumour activity. Senaparib at 5 mg/kg has same activity than olaparib at 100 mg/kg. PK analysis at 20 mg/kg demonstrated tumour drug concentrations were similar to plasma drug concentrations at the same dose. PAR expression levels were measured 2h after administration of senaparib 20 mg/kg, confirming target engagement. To conclude, this study demonstrated that senaparib diffused in the tumour at concentration sufficient to activate the target.

## 4.3.1.2. Secondary pharmacodynamics

Senaparib did not produce more than 50% inhibition or activation on any of the panel tested (receptors, ion channels and enzymes). In the same screening assay, metabolite M9 did inhibited two enzymes. At 10µM (100-fold the unbound Cmax of metabolite M9 97.3 nM), CYP450 3A4 and Peptidase/pepsin presented an inhibition of respectively of 55% and 68%. Human pepsin was inhibited only 13% at 50µM far from the clinical unbound concentration.

## 4.3.1.3. Safety pharmacology

Senaparib was tested in hERG study up to 10 µM (non-GLP compliant study). Although the IC50 was assumed to be higher than the highest tested dose, a dose-dependent inhibition was observed. The highest dose tested is represented only 36-fold the unbound clinical exposure (at posology of 100 mg senaparib daily, unbound Cmax = 131.8 ng/ml or 275 nM). At this dose, hERG channels were inhibited at 37%. A GLP study was performed later; however, the quality of this study is questionable. In addition, in dogs, cardiovascular effects were studied only up to 8 mg/kg. Although no TK measurements were performed in this safety pharmacology study, it can be assumed for PK data in 14-day study in dogs that dog exposures at this top dose of 8 mg/kg were far lower than the expected clinical exposure at the intended posology. Indeed, dog exposures based on total Cmax was 0.44-fold the clinical exposure and 0.75- fold based on unbound Cmax when adjustment is made with the protein binding. Given the results of these two studies conducted with senaparib, assessment of cardiovascular effect of senaparib was not satisfactory. Therefore, non-clinical data did not characterise the cardiac safety profile of senaparib. The risk of QT prolongation will be determined based only on the clinical experience (see clinical section).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Metabolite M9 was tested in hERG study up to 8.41 µM, 82-fold the unbound Cmax (GLP compliant study). No dose dependent effect is observed. A ramp instead of a step is used for eliciting tailcurrents for the metabolite testing. No conclusion could be drawn from the study design.

The effects of senaparib on central nervous system and respiratory function were studied in rats, with no treatment-related effects observed at doses up to 10 mg/kg. However, the rationale for selecting 10 mg/kg as the highest dose was not adequately explained, given that the Applicant considered the maximum tolerated dose following a single administration in rats to be at least 30 mg/kg. Furthermore, PK parameters were measured in these studies, and assumption of the multiples of animal exposures could be derived from the animal TK data measured in the dose-range finding studies. Indeed, in a 14-day dose-range-finding study in rats, PK parameters measured at D1 at 10 mg/kg showed AUC0-24 values of 884 ng.h/ml in males and 8070 ng.h/ml in females). Based on these data, the systemic exposures achieved following a single 10 mg/kg administration of senaparib in rats are estimated to correspond to 0.02-fold the clinical exposure in male patients at the MHRD and 0.19fold the clinical exposure in female patients at the MHRD. Consequently, the absence of toxic effects in these studies is not relevant for clinical extrapolation. In conclusion, because the exposures achieved at the doses tested in the rat and dog safety pharmacology studies were substantially below the clinical exposure at the MHRD the safety profile of senaparib should be based only on clinical experience.

## 4.3.1.4. Pharmacodynamic drug interactions

No studies were conducted.

## 4.3.2.1. Absorption

Senaparib had low permeability in the Caco-2 cell monolayer and was likely the substrate for efflux transporters. Dedicated PK studies were conducted in rats and in dogs after single or 7 day repeated dose administration. Mean oral bioavailability of senaparib was 12.8% in male rats, 44.4% in female rats, and 68.5% in dogs, showing significant sex differences in rats. Similar sex difference was observed in clearance (M/F 35.9/15.8 ml/min/kg) and half-life (M/F 0.83/3.11h). Senaparib showed no significant sex differences in beagle dogs, the mean plasma clearance was 6.85 ml/min/kg, the half-life 4.5h. No sex differences were observed in humans. After 7-day, 28-day, 26-week or 39-week repeated oral administration, the systemic exposure Cmax and AUC0-inf showed no accumulation in rats and dogs.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.3.2.2. Distribution

Senaparib was highly bound to plasma protein of mice (98.1%), monkeys (98.6%) and humans (96.1%) and moderate bound to plasma of dogs (88.2%) and rats (90.2%), with no concentration dependence effect. Tissue distribution of senaparib was investigated following single administration by oral gavage at 1 mg/kg of senaparib to male and female Sprague Dawley rats (3/sex/group). Concentrations of senaparib in plasma and tissue homogenate were determined by LC/MS/MS. The dose level of 1 mg/kg of senaparib was studied in the absorption PK study in rats; after one administration the observed Cmax was 34.8 ng/ml in male rats and 127 ng/ml in female rats. The rat exposure at this dose represents 0.01-fold the clinical exposure in men at the MHRD and 0.04-fold the clinical exposure in women at the MHRD. The selection of 1 mg/kg in this tissue distribution is not understood and considered not relevant to the clinical situation. In addition, the concentrations measured in plasma in the tissue distribution far lower than expected based on the absorption PK study. The number of the studied tissues in this standard tissue distribution is limited as well as the time duration of the study. The relevance of the tissue distribution study is very limited. A tissue distribution study (QWBA) in male Long Evans rats with 2mg/100 µCi/kg [ 14 C] was conducted with senaparib. Senaparib presented a limited blood brain barrier passage. Senaparib was mainly distributed in the gastrointestinal tract and liver. The vast majority of tissues peaked at 1 h post-dose and was rapidly eliminated thereafter, with concentrations in nearly half of the analysed tissues/AOIs falling below the limit of quantitation at 24 h post-dose. At 168 h post-dose (the last analysis time point), radioactivity was only detectable in the liver. In addition, senaparib was detectable in pigmented tissues. Specifically, radioactivity in the uveal tract remained quantifiable up to 24 h postdose, and the concentration in pigmented skin was higher than that in non-pigmented skin, indicating that senaparib bind to melanin, and this binding interaction is rapidly eliminated. The distribution across the placental transfer and excretion is the milk is not known. Distribution of metabolite M9 was not studied.

## 4.3.2.3. Metabolism

The metabolism studies of senaparib included three in vitro studies and two in vivo studies. In in vitro liver microsomes study, senaparib was mainly metabolized by oxidation, N-dealkylation, oxidative deamination, hydrogenation, defluorination and dehydrogenation; the major metabolites of oxidation were M9, M10, and M12, and the major metabolite of dehydrogenation (P - 2H) or oxidative dehydration (P + O - H2O) was M14. All metabolites detected in humans were detected in at least one animal species. CYP3A played a major role and other CYP isozymes (CYP1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19 and 2D6) played minor or no roles in the formations of M9, M10 and M12. Analysis of plasma samples after administration to rats and dogs revealed no metabolite detected in rat and only presence of M14 in dogs. No mass balance study was performed in the two species used in toxicology studies. The metabolism was studied in rats and dogs (MIST); this study allowed comparison of metabolite M9 and M14 after human and rat plasma samples analysis. In human plasma, senaparib, M9 and M14 were accounting for 79.21%, 20.35% and 0.44%. In rat plasma samples, senaparib, M9 and M14 were accounting for 99.42%, 5.15% and 3.43%.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In human mass balance study, the most abundant radioactive component in plasma was parent drug senaparib (accounting for 74.8% of the total radioactivity exposure in plasma), the main metabolite was M9 (accounting for 18.08%). Rats and dogs used in toxicology studies are not metabolically relevant species.

## 4.3.2.4. Excretion

Excretion was extensively studied in rats but not in dogs. Senaparib was demonstrated to be primarily excreted by faeces (82.5%) as the main excretion route, and urine (6.63%) as the minor excretion route in rats. The majority of radioactivity (89.1%) was recovered via urinary and faecal excretion within 72 hours after administration. The total amount excreted in bile within 72 hours after administration in BDC rats was 33.8% of the administered dose. No significant sex difference was observed.

## 4.3.2.5. Pharmacokinetic drug interactions

See clinical section.

## 4.3.2.6. Other pharmacokinetic studies

None.

## 4.4. Toxicology

The non-clinical toxicology program was performed according to ICH S9. Rat and dog are used in single and repeated-dose toxicity studies. The relevance of these two species is not explained. The toxicology profile was determined up to 26-week in rats and up to 39-week in dogs. The duration went beyond ICH S9 recommendations. A standard genotoxicity battery was conducted according to ICH S2 guideline. In accordance with ICH S9, no carcinogenicity studies were conducted with senaparib. No reprotoxicity studies with senaparib were conducted, reprotoxicity risk was determined based in a weight-of-evidence approach. A 3T3 NRU study was performed with senaparib. Metabolite M9 was determined as the major metabolite at more than 10 % in the mass balance study performed in humans. The pharmacology activity of this metabolite M9 was investigated (binding and potency) as well as the risk of torsade de pointe in a dedicated hERG study. A study to determine off-target activities was performed with metabolite M9. Protein binding was also determined, as well as potential DDI with metabolite M9 (see clinical part). No additional toxicity studies were performed with metabolite M9.

## 4.4.1. Single-dose toxicity

Single-dose studies were performed in dedicated studies in rats and dogs. In rats, the single dose study was conducted after the 14-day repeated dose toxicity study. The MTD was determined in rats to be higher than 30 mg/kg. In dogs, two studies were performed but results are discordant. The dose selected in the first single dose phase in dogs was based on the tolerance observed at 2.4 mg/kg in PK study. In this first study, the MTD was determined to be 24 mg/kg since a female died at Day 17 after a single administration of 24 mg/kg senaparib, the relation to the test article is uncertain but could not be excluded. The second study was performed as the first phase of a combined single and 14-day toxicity study. In this second study, no death was observed up to 24.4 mg/kg, only adverse haematological effect was observed at this highest dose. Therefore, the MTD in this study is 24.4 mg/kg. No TK analysis was performed after single administration and no multiple of exposures were presented.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.4.2. Repeat-dose toxicity

Repeat-dose toxicity studies were conducted in rats and dogs following oral administration of senaparib. The programme included non-GLP 14-day studies in both species, GLP 4-week studies with 4-week recovery periods, and longer-term GLP studies of 26 weeks in rats and 39 weeks in dogs. In rats, the 14-day study included dose levels of 5, 7.5 and 10 mg/kg/day. Mortality or moribundity occurred in females at all dose levels, including animals from the toxicokinetic groups, and all females at 10 mg/kg/day were euthanised on Day 13. Findings included reduced body weight and food consumption in females at 10 mg/kg/day; decreases in red and white blood cell parameters in males at 10 mg/kg/day and females at ≥5 mg/kg/day; changes in coagulation and serum chemistry in females at 10 mg/kg/day; and organ weight changes in adrenal glands, liver, spleen and thymus. Histopathological findings included decreased haematopoietic cellularity in bone marrow, Kupffer cell hypertrophy and increased hepatocyte mitosis in liver, haemoglobin crystals and bacterial colonies in lungs, adrenal cortical hypertrophy/congestion/haemorrhage, and lymphoid depletion in immune organs. In the 4-week rat study, dose levels were 0.5, 1 and 2 mg/kg/day followed by a 4-week recovery period. No mortality or clinical observations were reported. Findings at 2 mg/kg/day included reduced body weight and food consumption in females, reduced food consumption in males, pale/discoloured eyes in females, haematological changes in females, decreased thymus weight in females and minimal lymphoid depletion in mandibular and/or mesenteric lymph nodes. The reported NOAELs were 2 mg/kg/day in males and 1 mg/kg/day in females. In the 26-week rat study, dose levels were 0.5, 1 and 2 mg/kg/day followed by a 4-week recovery period. Two early deaths occurred in females at 2 mg/kg/day, one on Day 43 and one on Day 131. Findings at 2 mg/kg/day in females included discoloured skin, reduced body weight and food consumption, and haematological changes affecting red and white blood cell parameters and platelets. No treatment-related histopathological findings were reported. The reported NOAELs were 2 mg/kg/day in males and 1 mg/kg/day in females. In dogs, the 14-day study included dose levels of 0.73, 2.4 and 7.3 mg/kg/day. One male at 7.3 mg/kg/day was found dead on Day 10, and one male at 2.4 mg/kg/day was euthanised moribund on Day 12. Animals at 7.3 mg/kg/day were euthanised on Day 11 and animals at 2.4 mg/kg/day on Day 13 due to clinical toxicity. Findings included abnormal stool, decreased activity, inappetence, thin appearance, decreased body weight and food consumption, haematological changes, increased fibrinogen and prothrombin time, serum chemistry changes, thymus weight changes, Kupffer cell hypertrophy and hepatocellular glycogen depletion in liver, thymic lymphoid depletion, and lung/mandibular lymph node findings in one male at 2.4 mg/kg/day. The reported NOAEL was below 0.73 mg/kg/day. In the 4-week dog study, dose levels were 0.2, 0.4 and 0.8 mg/kg/day followed by a 4-week recovery period. At 0.8 mg/kg/day, one male and one female were euthanised moribund on Days 16 and 26, respectively, with findings primarily associated with marked bone marrow hypocellularity. Abnormal stool was observed in all dose groups. Haematological changes were observed during dosing. Histopathological findings included bone marrow hypocellularity in males at ≥0.4 mg/kg/day and females at 0.8 mg/kg/day, and lymphoid depletion in lymphoid tissues. The reported HNSTD was 0.4 mg/kg/day. period. No mortality occurred. Clinical observations included skin findings such as rash, discolouration,

In the 39-week dog study, dose levels were 0.1, 0.2 and 0.4 mg/kg/day followed by a 4-week recovery alopecia, scabs, excessive scratching and skin scaling, as well as abnormal stool. One female at 0.4

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

mg/kg/day had minimal retinal dysplasia. Haematological changes were observed at 0.2 and 0.4 mg/kg/day. Coagulation changes included increased fibrinogen. Histopathological findings at 0.4 mg/kg/day included minimal thymic atrophy, bone marrow hypocellularity or hypercellularity, and skin inflammation/infiltration; findings during recovery included skin inflammation, bone marrow hypocellularity and thymic atrophy. The reported HNSTD was 0.4 mg/kg/day.

Across the repeat-dose toxicity studies, toxicokinetic analyses showed higher exposure in female rats than males, no sex-related exposure difference in dogs, no accumulation after repeated dosing, and generally dose-related increases in exposure.

## 4.4.3. Genotoxicity

Senaparib showed no mutagenic potential in an Ames test, senaparib is positive for chromosomal aberrations in vitro in mammalian cells and in vivo following oral dosing to rats. Carcinogenicity

In accordance with ICH S9, no carcinogenicity studies were conducted with senaparib.

## 4.4.4. Developmental and reproductive toxicity

No reproductive and developmental toxicity was conducted with senaparib. The applicant performed a weight-of-evidence assessment taking into consideration literature data highlighting a critical role of the primary pharmacological targets in embryogenesis, as well as the toxicological profile of compounds with a similar mode of action shown to induce embryolethality and/or malformations in rats.

In line with ICH S9, no fertility or pre/postnatal toxicity studies were conducted.

Product-specific waivers were granted for development of senaparib in all subsets of the paediatric population in the applied conditions. Accordingly, no juvenile animal studies were conducted.

## 4.4.5. Toxicokinetics and exposure margins

See above.

## 4.4.6. Local tolerance

No local tolerance studies to access alternate routes is performed.

## 4.4.7. Other toxicity studies

## Immunotoxicity

According to ICH S8, anti-proliferative agents used to treat cancer are an example of drugs that produce unintended immunosuppression. In such instances, adverse findings in nonclinical studies are predictive of human immunotoxicity in a rather straightforward manner. Indeed, the results obtained from the general toxicity studies in rats and dogs indicate that senaparib causes haematological toxicity which leads to decreases in circulating white and red blood cells and changes in platelet counts. Infections and septicaemia observed particularly in animals were a consequence of bone marrow suppression and lymphoid depletion. These adverse findings are considered related to the mechanism of action of senaparib.

## Metabolite M9

Senaparib is mainly metabolized by CYP3A4 to form a major active metabolite M9 (mono-oxidation of piperazine ring metabolite). Metabolite M9 was determined as the major metabolite at more than 10 %

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

in the mass balance study performed in humans (human total Cmax 633 ng/ml, unbound Cmax = 48 ng/ml = 97.3 nM), probably detected late during the drug development and the toxicology program was not pursued. The submitted data are showing that metabolite M9 is not present in rat or dog plasma, therefore rats and dogs used in toxicology studies are not metabolically relevant species. The PD activity of metabolite M9 was studied. Metabolite M9 is an active metabolite at the clinical exposure and is participating to the overall engagement. At 10µM (100-fold the unbound Cmax of metabolite M9 97.3 nM), CYP450 3A4 and peptidase/pepsin presented an inhibition of respectively of 55% and 68% (see above). Metabolite M9 was tested in hERG study up to 8.41 µM, 82-fold the unbound Cmax (GLP compliant study). No dose dependent effect is observed. No additional genotoxicity and reprotoxicity studies were performed with the M9 metabolite. This is acceptable, considering that senaparib is genotoxic, and in view of the intended patient population. Metabolite M9 is also be assumed to be genotoxic given its structure and its pharmacological activity.

## Phototoxicity

Senaparib showed little absorbance within the UV range. Senaparib absorbs only in UVc and not in UV B or A or visible light. This in vitro study 3T3 NRU showed non-phototoxic potential up to 8.3 μg/mL.

## 4.5. Overall discussion and conclusions on non-clinical aspects

## 4.5.1. Discussion

## Pharmacology

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Senaparib inhibits both PARP-1 and PARP-2, both implicated in DNA damage repair but senaparib also inhibits PARP5A/B, PARP6, PARP7, PARP10, PARP11, PARP12 and PARP15 at the clinical exposure at the MHRD. It is not clear if the binding is related to the intended on-target effect or off-target activity. Senaparib showed slightly more PARP1/PARP2 inhibition potency than olaparib, niraparib, and rucaparib. Cytotoxicity assays in a panel of cancer cell lines (including ovarian cancer, BRCA-mutant and wt, HRD and HRP cell lines) showed that senaparib was only slightly more cytotoxic toward BRCA-mutant (HRD) than BRCAwt HRP cell lines, and more cytotoxic than niraparib and rucaparib in all tested cell lines. Senaparib PARP trapping activity was more potent than olaparib in an HRD prostate cancer cell line and more potent than niraparib and rucaparib in either BRCA mutant HRD TNBC or BRCAwt HRP ovarian cancer cell line. Senaparib achieved similar tumour regression with a much lower dose than niraparib and rucaparib in BRCA1-mutant TNBC xenograft model. Senaparib did not produce more than 50% inhibition or activation on any of the panel tested. The submitted hERG study was not GLP-compliant, the high dose tested is not justified, a dose dependent inhibition of hERG was observed and the multiple of exposure is limited. An additional GLP hERG study was submitted but the quality of the study is questionable. In addition, the tested doses in the safety pharmacology studies in rats and dogs are far below the clinical exposure at the MHRD, therefore safety profile of senaparib could be based only on clinical experience. Pharmacokinetics Dedicated PK studies were performed in rats and dogs. Senaparib presented a higher protein binding in humans than in rats and dogs. Distribution study was performed and senaparib presented a limited blood brain barrier passage and was mainly distributed in the gastrointestinal tract and liver. Metabolite M9 was determined as the major metabolite at more than 10 % in the mass balance study performed in humans, probably late during the drug development and the toxicology program was not pursued. The submitted data are showing that metabolite M9 is not present in rat or dog plasma, therefore rats and dogs used in toxicology studies are not metabolically relevant species. As in humans, faeces were the major excretion pathway, while urine was a minor route of excretion in rats. Toxicology The non-clinical program was performed according to ICH S9, which is acceptable. Rat and dog are used in single and repeated-dose toxicity studies. The toxicology profile was determined up to 26-week in rats and up to 39-week in dogs. In repeat dose toxicity studies in rats and dogs, haematological toxicity was dose limiting. Even at short term treatment, no clinical exposure could be achieved. The pursuit of the non-clinical program at 4 weeks and even more at long term 26/39 weeks is questionable from 3Rs perspectives. Indeed, in chronic toxicity studies, at the highest doses in rats and dogs, animal exposures were only &lt;0.01-fold the clinical exposure at the MHRD. Animal data are therefore not sufficient to extrapolate the safety profile in clinical setting. The clinical experience is currently sufficient to not conduct additional animal studies with senaparib or its metabolite M9. In addition, toxic findings were observed in bone marrow (rat and dog), spleen (rat and dog), thymus (rat and dog), adrenal gland (in rat and dog), liver (in rat and dog), lung (in rat and dog), eye (in rat and dog), skin (in rat and dog) and testis (in dogs) at exposures far lower than the clinical exposure at the MHRD. Toxic findings observed in the spleen, thymus, adrenal glands, lungs and liver are considered secondary to the bone marrow inhibition. Erythrocyte count, leukocyte count and lymphoid decreased resulted in secondary effects caused by weakened immunity. PARP trapping is involved in bone marrow

toxicity; therefore, these toxicity findings are consistent with the pharmacology of senaparib. The observed skin toxicity (discolour skin) may be related to the inflammatory response. The relationship of the dysplasia in retinal with optical disc edge unclear observed in one dog with senaparib remains unclear.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The animal findings are adequately reported in section 5.3 of the SmPC. and in the non-clinical part of the RMP and their relevance in humans is reflected (see also discussion on Clinical Pharmacology).

A standard genotoxicity battery was conducted according to ICH S2 guideline. Senaparib is positive for chromosomal aberrations in vitro in mammalian cells and in vivo following oral dosing to rats. This is expected based on the pharmacological activity resulting in impairment of DNA repair. This entails that a potential for genotoxicity and carcinogenicity in humans cannot be excluded. This is correctly reflected in the SmPC section 5.3 subsection genotoxicity and in the RMP Part II: Module SII.

In accordance with ICH S9, no carcinogenicity studies were conducted with senaparib. This is considered acceptable and the lack of animal carcinogenicity is adequately reflected in the SmPC section 5.3. Due to the pharmacological activity, the second primary malignancy is associated with treatments involving DNA-damage agents as senaparib. Indeed, PARP inhibition increases the overall burden of single-strand and double-strand breaks in all dissociation cells, which is the basis of potential risk for the development of a second primary malignancy. This risk of second primary malignancy (other than MDS/AML) is classified in the safety concerns as an important potential risk in the RMP.

PARP inhibition increases the overall burden of single-strand and double-strand breaks in all dissociation cells, which is the basis of potential risk for the development of a second primary malignancy. No reproductive toxicity studies with senaparib were conducted, the risk of reproductive toxicity was determined based on a weight-of-evidence approach. On the basis of a weight-of-evidence assessment, considering the critical role of PARP1 and PARP2 in embryogenesis and toxicological profile of compounds with a similar mode of action, embryotoxic and teratogenic effects are anticipated with senaparib. Therefore, the conduct of additional embryofoetal development studies with senaparib is not deemed necessary. The text for SmPC sections 4.4 and 4.6 indicates that Sepalna should not be used during pregnancy and in women of childbearing potential who are not using contraception. A pregnancy test is required before initiation of the treatment. In addition, SmPC section 4.6 provides information for prescribers on the nature of adverse developmental effects that may be expected based on the mechanism of action.

Relevant information on potential effects of senaparib on fertility could not be obtained from repeatdose toxicity data due to exposure levels representing 1-2% of human exposure. However, data available with other approved PARP inhibitors and literature data indicate a potential for adverse effects on human fertility. Women of childbearing potential are recommended to use highly effective contraception for up to 6 months after receiving the last dose of Sepalna, in accordance with the NcWP/SWP recommendations for genotoxic drugs. . Furthermore, as an additional precaution, given the uncertainty regarding the potential for Sepalna to reduce the effectiveness of hormonal contraceptives, SmPC sections 4.4 and 4.5 state that women using systemically acting hormonal contraceptives should also use a barrier method during treatment and for at least 6 months after the last dose. No additional genotoxicity and reproductive toxicity studies were requested, metabolite M9 is assumed to be genotoxic. A 3T3 NRU study performed with senaparib was negative, no phototoxic potential is detected.

## 4.5.2. Conclusions

Overall, the non-clinical data support the pharmacological activity of senaparib as a PARP inhibitor and demonstrate antitumour activity in relevant models. The main toxicities, particularly haematological and bone marrow effects, are consistent with the mechanism of action. The genotoxic, carcinogenic and reproductive risks are adequately addressed in the SmPC and RMP. From a non-clinical point of view, the application could be approvable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5. Clinical aspects

## 5.1. Introduction

## 5.1.1. Good Clinical Practice (GCP) aspects

The applicant has provided a statement to the effect that clinical trials conducted outside the community were carried out in accordance with the ethical standards of Directive 2001/20/EC.

Based on the review of clinical data, CHMP did not identify the need for a further GCP inspection of the clinical trials included in this dossier (see section 2.4.3. ).

A request for routine GCP inspection has been adopted in September 2025 for the following clinical study: IMP4297-301. The outcome of this inspection and the satisfactory responses to its findings were provided by the applicant.
