## 5.2. Clinical pharmacology

Senaparib (IMP4297), a new chemical entity, is an orally administered, poly (ADP-ribose) polymerase (PARP) inhibitor.

In the current submission, the applicant seeks marketing approval for senaparib, for the maintenance treatment of adult patients with advanced epithelial (FIGO Stages III and IV) high-grade ovarian, fallopian tube or primary peritoneal cancer who are in response (complete or partial) following completion of first-line platinum-based chemotherapy.

The proposed recommended oral dose is 100 mg QD taken as five 20-mg capsules.

Senaparib has a molecular weight of 514.48 Da. The molecular formula is C24H20F2N6O3●2H2O and the chemical structure is presented in Figure 5. Senaparib has no chiral center.

Figure 2: Structure of senaparib drug substance

<!-- image -->

Overall 22 non-clinical pharmacokinetic studies (i.e. in vitro permeability assessment, in vitro metabolite profiling) as listed in Table 5 and Table 8 were conducted. The clinical pharmacology program for senaparib presented in Table 9, encompasses 11 completed clinical studies, 8 performed in healthy volunteers (see below), 3 in patients with advanced solid tumour or ovarian cancer (IMP4297 -2016CN01, IMP4297 -2016-AU01 and IMP4297 -2018-CN01), as well as one ongoing Phase 3 study IMP4297-301 (pivotal study):

- One mass balance and metabolism study (IMP4297-109)
- One biopharmaceutical study investigating the effect of food on senaparib PK (IMP4297-103) and five rBA studies (IMP4297-107/108/110-1/110-2/113)
- One DDI study (IMP4297-104).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In addition, a population PK analysis for senaparib and its main metabolite M9 and Exposure-Response analyses were developed.

## 5.2.1. Methods

## 5.2.1.0. Bioanalysis

Several bioanalytical methods were developed throughout the clinical development to quantify senaparib or its main metabolites (M9 and M14) in human plasma by LC-MS/MS techniques. Pharmacokinetic data analysis

For single or multiple-dose studies, PK parameters evaluated in plasma include Cmax, Clast, Cmin, Tmax, Tlast, AUCs (AUC0-t, AUC0-24h, AUC0-∞), CL/F and Vz/F, AUC%extra, AI (accumulation index), Kel, λz, T1/2. For the mass balance study, additional PK parameters as MPAUC, MPCmax (MP for metabolite/parent ratio), Ae and fe (amount and fraction excreted) were estimated.

Standard non-compartmental (model-independent) pharmacokinetic methods were used to calculate PK parameters using Phoenix WinNonlin v8.2 or later.

## 5.2.1.1. Population analysis

Two population pharmacokinetic (Pop-PK) analyses were conducted based on PK data from the same three studies: two Phase 1 studies (IMP4297-2016-AU01 in Australia and IMP4297-2016-CN01 in China) and one Phase 2 study (IMP4297-2018-CN01 in China), in patients with advanced solid tumours.

Data processing, exploratory analyses, dataset assembly, and graphical presentation were performed using SAS software version 9.4 and R version 4.1.3. Pop-PK modelling was performed in NONMEM version 7.5.0, with PsN version 4.8.1 as an auxiliary tool. The FOCEI algorithm was applied for parameter estimation. The base model was derived from the previously established 2023 Pop-PK model (a two-compartment structure with absorption lag time [ALAG1], first-order absorption, and first-order elimination). Effects of formulation and body weight were assessed at this stage. Interindividual variability (IIV) was modelled exponentially, and residual error structures (proportional, additive, and combined) were evaluated. Covariates were explored using a stepwise covariate modelling (SCM) procedure, combining forward selection (p&lt;0.01) and backward elimination (p&lt;0.001).

Model performance was assessed through diagnostic goodness-of-fit plots and prediction-corrected visual predictive checks (pcVPC). Parameter precision and model robustness were further evaluated by bootstrap analyses, while η- and ε-shrinkage were examined to assess reliability of individual parameter estimates.ER analysis

An integrated exposure-response (E-R) analysis of senaparib in patients with advanced solid tumours (LTT-08435-Senaparib\\_E-R\\_PMAR, version date 01-Jul-2025) was performed using clinical data pooled from three studies: IMP4297-2016-AU01 (Phase I, N=39), IMP4297-2016-CN01 (Phase I, N=57), and IMP4297-2018-CN01 (Phase II, N=93; data cut-off 17-Dec-2024).

Exposure-efficacy analyses were conducted in 128 patients with ovarian cancer (investigatorevaluated, pooled across the three studies); pre-specified subgroup analyses included 92 investigatorevaluated and 91 IRC-evaluated patients from study IMP4297-2018-CN01. Exposure-safety analyses were carried out on the pooled dataset of 189 patients from the three studies.

Exposure metrics used for the E-R analyses (Cmax, Cmin and AUC24 at steady state) were individual patient estimates derived from the final Pop-PK model and were calculated based on the patient's average daily dose (AVEDOSE). A stepwise approach was applied, beginning with graphical inspection followed by logistic regression modelling.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Graphical exploratory analyses included stacked histograms, boxplots, ROC curves and Kaplan-Meier stratifications to inspect relationships between exposure and BOR/ORR/TTR/DOR/PFS and safety endpoints. Formal E-R modelling for categorical endpoints (ORR; ≥Grade-3 TEAEs and TEAEH) used logistic regression with the drug effect parameterized as either a linear or Emax function on the logit scale; categorical models were estimated using the LAPLACE method and implemented in NONMEM v7.5.0 with PsN v4.8.1 as auxiliary software. Data assembly and exploratory graphics were performed using SAS v9.4 and R (v4.1.3 or later). Model diagnostics included standard GOF checks and VPCs.

## 5.2.2. Pharmacokinetics

## 5.2.2.1. Absorption

## Healthy volunteers

In both Chinese and Australian healthy volunteers, absorption was rapid and generally achieved at a Tmax of 2h.

In Chinese healthy volunteers, following a single dose of 100 mg senaparib (5x20 mg) as MF20C, geometric mean Cmax was 1421 ng/mL and AUCinf was 9962 ng.h/mL (Study IMP4297-110-1).

In Australian healthy volunteers, following a single dose of 100 mg senaparib (5x20 mg) as MF20, geometric mean Cmax was 1290 ng/mL and AUCinf was 8820 ng.h/mL (Study IMP4297-107).

## Patients

In both Chinese and Australian patients with advanced solid tumours, absorption was rapid and generally achieved at a Tmax of 2h.

In Chinese patients with advanced tumours, following single dose of 100 mg senaparib as MF20, geometric mean Cmax was 2965 ng/mL and AUCt was 35154 ng.h/mL and following multiple dose geometric mean Cmax was 3213 ng/mL and AUCt was 33827 ng.h/mL (Study IMP4297-2016-CN01).

In Australian patients with advanced tumours, following single dose of 100 mg senaparib as MF20, geometric mean Cmax was 2645 ng/mL and AUCt was 24692 ng.h/mL and following multiple dose geometric mean Cmax was 3377 ng/mL and AUCt was 41524 ng.h/mL (Study IMP4297-2016-AU01).

In the target population, following multiple doses of 100 mg (10x10mg) senaparib as MF10C, predicted geometric mean Cmaxss was 2629 ng/mL and AUCtauss was 25355 ng.h/mL (PPK analysis).

## Absolute bioavailability

A formal absolute bioavailability study in humans has not been performed and an IV formulation was not developed for use in humans. The absolute oral bioavailability in beagle dogs was 68.5% (see NC section).

## BCS classification

Based on in vitro investigations on solubility and permeability, senaparib can be classified as a BCS class 4 compound (low solubility/low permeability).

## 5.2.2.2. Bioequivalence

Several oral formulations of senaparib were developed and evaluated during the clinical development program, for which bridging studies vs the claimed commercial formulation MF20C have been performed.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.2.2.3. Influence of food

The effect of a standardized high-fat, high-calorie meal and fasting on senaparib PK was investigated in two studies (Study IMP4297-103 and Study IMP4297-110-2).

From study IMP4297-103, following a single dose of senaparib of 100 mg (10x10 mg as MF10), after a high-fat meal, Tmax was delayed by 3h (fast vs fed: 2h vs 5h), Cmax was reduced by 24% and both AUCs increased by 24% (AUCt) and 28% (AUCinf).

By indirect comparison of the PK data from studies IMP4297-110-1 (fast 100 mg) and IMP4297-110-2 (fed 100 mg) performed with the commercial formulation (MF20C), in the fed state, Tmax was delayed by 2.5 h, Cmax increased by 1.23-fold (1755 vs 1421 ng/mL) and AUCinf by 1.82.

## 5.2.2.4. Distribution

Senaparib has a mean 94.5% protein binding on AAG, a B/P ratio of 0.54 and an estimated Vz/F in Australian patients of 50.6 L and in Chinese patients 39.6 L.

M9 has a mean protein binding of 94.5% on AAG.

## 5.2.2.5. Metabolism

Metabolite profiling was performed and 12 metabolites were identified in total excreta and 8 in plasma (Figure 6). In plasma senaparib was the most prominent drug related component and accounted for 74.8% of TRA for pooled AUC0-48h. M9 a direct Phase 1 metabolite involving CYP3A4 accounted for 18.08% of TRA for pooled AUC0-48h.

In faeces, senaparib was the main component excreted in faeces with 47.38% of the dose, which can be attributed to pre-systemic elimination given its solubility limited absorption. M9 accounted for 15.4% and other metabolites for less than 5%. More than 92% of the dose excreted in faeces was identified.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In urine, unchanged senaparib was found at a low level (1.2%). M266 was the main component excreted with 2.54% of the dose. M266 was formed by N-dealkylation of AGI-69460. Less than 80% of the dose excreted in urine was identified (70%).

Figure 3: Possible major metabolic pathways of senaparib

<!-- image -->

- Interconversion

Senaparib is achiral.

- Pharmacokinetics of metabolites

PK characterization of two metabolites M9 and M14 were performed across the clinical development.

M9 was a direct Phase 1 metabolite, identified as a main metabolite accounting for 18.08% of TRA for pooled AUC0-48h (Study IMP4297-109) and formed following the involvement of CYP3A4.

M9 is an active metabolite (Report IMPA-20190428) with IC50 of 2.54 nM, 1.29 nM on PARP1 and PARP2 respectively (6.27 nM, 1.57 nM, 9.67 nM and 43.76 nM on PARP1, 2, 3a and 6 respectively for senaparib). M9 contributes to 29% to the pharmacological activity on both PARPs. M9 has a lower cytotoxic effect against the growth of human breast cancer cell lines with BRCA1 mutation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

From study IMP4297-2018-CN01 geomean predicted PK parameters, AUC24h, Cmaxss and Cminss, for 84 patients receiving 100 mg QD of senaparib at steady-state, were 8805 ng.h/mL, 633 ng/mL and 197 ng/mL, respectively. Predicted effective half-life was 10.8 h.

M14 is a minor metabolite and was characterized as part of studies 104 and 109.

## 5.2.2.6. Elimination

In studies IMP4297-2016-CN01 and IMP4297-2016-AU01 following a 100 mg dose of senaparib (modified formulation, MF20) CL/F was ~3 L/h and ~3.5 L/h, respectively and T1/2 was 10h and 11.9 h, respectively. Following multiple doses of 100 mg senaparib, at steady-state, CL/F was 3.5 L/h and 4.4 L/h, respectively and T1/2 was 8.3h and ~7h, respectively.

Based on the PPK analysis, CL/F was estimated at ~3.8 L/h. Senaparib is highly metabolized and cleared by faecal excretion.

## · Mass balance

The excretion and biotransformation of [ 14 C]-senaparib was investigated in 6 healthy Chinese volunteers following a single oral dose of 100 mg senaparib (suspension) incorporating approximately 100 µCi [ 14 C]-senaparib, in the fed state.

The total recovery of radioactivity was approximately 99% of the dose. Approximately 84.55% and 15.18% of the dose was recovered in faeces and urine respectively, unchanged senaparib accounted for 47.38% and 1.2% in faeces and urine respectively. Most of the administered radioactivity (86.1%) was recovered in the first 72h post-dose (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Cumulative excretion rate curves in urine and faeces, and total recovery curve

<!-- image -->

5.2.2.7. Dose proportionality and time dependency

Senaparib exposure increase less than dose proportionality from 80 mg to 150 mg, suggesting a trend toward absorption saturation at higher doses (&gt;80 mg). Dose proportionality has not been established within 2-80 mg.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Steady-state has been reached by C1D15. At a 100 mg dose in Australian and Chinese, the accumulation ratio was 1.43 and 1.17 respectively.

## 5.2.2.8. Intra- and inter-individual variability

The inter-subject variability in exposure of senaparib in health volunteers across studies in fasting conditions was moderate with range from 16 to 41% for Cmax and 24 to 42% for AUCinf. In patients with advanced solid tumours, similar range are observed 37 to 44% for Cmax and 47 to 50% for AUCinf (Table 11).

| Vz/F (L)            | 50.6a (32%)                         | 39.6b (28)                          | 81.7 (41%)        | 120 (45%)         | 135 (42%)         | 123 (39%) d       | 149 (36%)         |
| t1/2 (h)            | 611 (68%)                           | q666                                | 6.20 (25%)        | 8.66 (42%)        | 8.35 (62%) c      | 7.49 (35%)d       | 9.97 (35%)        |

Based on the PPK analysis IIV CL/F and Vc/F were 57% and 90.7% respectively.

The intra-subject variability of senaparib based on CV% from crossover studies was generally moderate ranging from 13.4% to 18.7% for Cmax and from 16% to 19% for AUCinf.

## 5.2.2.9. Pharmacokinetics in the target population

Four clinical studies have investigated senaparib PK in patients with advanced solid tumour (IMP4297-2016-CN/AU01) and in advanced ovarian cancer subjects (IMP4297-2018-CN01 and IMP4297-301).

No PK data are available for the pivotal study IMP4297-301.

In study IMP4297-2018-CN01, a total of 93 subjects were included to receive continuous dose of 100 mg senaparib (28 days, 1 cycle) administered as a capsule (MF10 and MF10C) in the fasted state. In case of TEAEs, dose reduction was allowed from 100 mg to 40 mg (20 mg step decrease) and only 41 patients had not dose reduction.

Based on the PPK model, geomean Cmaxss, Cminss and AUC24ss were predicted at 2629 ng/mL, 293 ng/mL and 25355 ng.h/mL respectively (Table 14). CL/F, Vd/F and T1/2eff were estimated at 3.95 L/h, 27.3 L and 8.86 h.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.2.2.10. Population Pharmacokinetics analysis

## 5.2.2.10.1. Senaparib

The analysis included PK data from 3 clinical studies: 2 Phase І studies (IMP4297-2016-AU01 and IMP4297-2016-CN01 conducted in Australia and China in patients with advanced solid tumours, respectively, doses of 2-150 mg once daily QD or 50 mg twice daily BID) and 1 pivotal Phase II study IMP4297-2018-CN01 conducted in China in patients with advanced ovarian cancer carrying BRCA 1/2 mutation administered 100 mg QD (cut-off date, Dec 17, 2024).

Overall, the Pop-PK dataset included 2615 PK senaparib concentrations obtained from 189 patients. Among them, 186 PK observations were below the quantitation limit (BQL) (including 91 post-dose PK observations (3.5%)), 1 PK observation was non-BQL before the first dose and the concentration of 1 record was missing. These 188 PK observations (~7%) were excluded from this analysis. Finally, 2427 PK observations from 189 patients were used for this analysis.

The final Pop-PK model consisted of a two-compartment model with sequential zero- and first-order absorption, and first-order elimination. IIV was estimated on all parameters (Ka, D1, Vc/F, Vp/F and CL/F) except for Q/F. RUV was modelled using a proportional error. The significant covariates impacting PK of senaparib were: the effect of drug formulation (DFORM: original vs. modified) on the relative bioavailability F1, with a coefficient of 1.78 (reference: modified formulation). the effects of BW on clearances and volume of distribution terms, with estimated allometric scaling exponents of 0.478 (CL/F, Q/F) and 0.606 (Vc/F, Vp/F), respectively and the effect of baseline albumin (BALB) on CL/F (with a coefficient of 1.26).

Table 12 provides the PK parameter estimates of the final Pop-PK model of senaparib and results of shrinkage and the bootstrap analysis.

The basic goodness-of-fit diagnostic plots for the final Pop-PK model are presented in Figure 5. The prediction-corrected visual predictive checks (pcVPC) stratified by study and BW are shown in Figure 6 and Figure 7, respectively.

In a typical patient (BW 65 kg, ALB 44g/L, under modified formulation), CL/F and Vc/F and Vp/F were estimated to be 3.76 L/h, 3.07 L and 24.7 L, respectively. The forest plots of significant and nonsignificant covariates on senaparib exposure parameters at steady state (AUC24ss, Cmaxss Cminss) after administration of 100 mg QD are presented in Figure 8 and Figure 9. The summary of steadystate exposure parameters and percentages relative to the typical subject for subjects at different covariate levels are also shown in Table 13.

## 5.2.2.10.2. Metabolite M9

A total of 526 PK observations from 84 subjects from Phase II study (IMP4297-2018-CN01) were included in M9 Pop-PK analysis.

The fraction metabolized from senaparib to M9 (Fm), was fixed at 18.1% in the clinical study report for the mass balance study IMP4297-109 due to parameter identifiability issues during modelling. A twocompartment model with first-order generation and first-order elimination could adequately characterize the pharmacokinetic profile of M9 in patients with advanced ovarian cancer in the Phase II study (IMP4297-2018-CN01). The final model did not include any covariates; that is, the base model and the final model were the same.

Parameter estimates of the Pop-PK final model for metabolite M9 are presented in Table 15 and the corresponding basic goodness-of-fit diagnostic plots and (pcVPC) are presented in Figure 11 and Figure 12, respectively.

## 5.2.2.11. Special populations

## Renal impairment

Results from the mass balance study indicated that excretion in the urine is minor (15%), suggesting minimal renal elimination. No dedicated study was conducted to assess the impact of renal impairment on senaparib PK.

The effect of renal impairment as measured by the estimated glomerular function rate (eGFR): normal [n=89, 47.1%], mild [n=75, 39.7%], moderate [n=24, 12.7%]) was evaluated in the Pop-PK analysis. Renal impairment was not identified as a significant covariate on the PK parameters of senaparib (Figure 12).

There were no data for patients with severe renal impairment or end-stage renal failure.

## Hepatic impairment

No dedicated study was conducted to assess the impact of hepatic impairment on senaparib PK.

According to the National Cancer Institute-Organ Dysfunction Working Group (NCI-ODWG), the analysed Pop-PK dataset included 165 (87.3%) subjects with normal hepatic function, 24 (12.7%) with mild hepatic impairment. The Pop-PK analysis showed that hepatic impairment did not have significant effects on the PK parameters of senaparib.

There were no data for patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment.

## Gender

The influence of gender on the PK of senaparib was evaluated using the Pop-PK approach.

The analysis included more females (163/189, ~86.3%) than males (26/189, ~13.7%). Analysis of covariates showed that gender was not as a statistically significant covariate on senaparib CL/F in the final model.

## Ethnicity

The majority of patients were Asian (n= 152 of 198, 80%), with n=35 patients being White (20%) and n= 2 patients classified as other racial groups. The effect of race was evaluated as a categorical covariate in the Pop-PK analysis and race was not retained as a statistically significant covariate on the PK parameters of senaparib in the final model.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The summary of model-based simulated steady-state exposure parameters (AUC24ss, Cmaxss and Cminss) of senaparib in patients with advanced solid tumours at 100 mg QD by race (Asian vs. White) is presented in Table 17. The boxplots of simulated steady-state exposure parameters by race are presented in Figure 13. The results showed substantial overlap between the two groups, with slightly lower median values in White patients (88%, 91%, and 90% of Asian values, respectively).

<!-- image -->

Cmaxss: peak concentration at steady state; Cminss: trough concentration at steady state; AUC24ss: area under concentration-time curve over a 24 h dosing interval at steady state; QD: once daily.

The weight of Asian and White patients (mean±SD) was 62.9±8.8 kg and 73.6±14.1 kg, respectively.

Boxplots represent median, interquartile range (IQR), Q1-1.5·IQR (lower whiskers), and Q3+1.5·IQR (upper whiskers) of exposure, with points outside the whiskers being outliers.

## Bodyweight

No formal investigations with regard to body weight (BW) has been performed. The effect of BW on senaparib PK was investigated as part of the Pop-PK analysis.

Among the 189 patients included in the dataset, the median BW was 63 kg (min = 42 kg, max = 103 kg). In the Pop-PK analysis, the effect of BW was retained as a statistically significant covariate on clearances and volume of distribution terms, with estimated allometric scaling exponents of 0.478 (for CL/F, Q/F) and 0.606 (for Vc/F, Vp/F). This suggests that clearance of senaparib increases with increasing BW, and thus, systemic exposure is higher in individuals with lower BW.

Based on Pop-PK model simulations, no significant differences (&gt;±20%) in senaparib exposure were observed across the range of BW values and consequently no dosing adjustment is warranted based on BW.

## Elderly

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

No formal investigations with regard to age has been performed. The effect of age on senaparib PK was investigated as part of the Pop-PK analysis.

The mean age of the 189 patients included in the population dataset was 56.9 years (median = 56 years, min = 31 years, max = 83 years). The majority of patients were under 65 years of age (n = 145, 76.7%), while 44 patients (23.3%) were aged ≥65 years or older.

The effect of age was not retained as a significant covariate impacting the PK parameters of senaparib.

## Paediatric population

The PK, safety and efficacy of senaparib in children and adolescents under 18 years of age have not been established.

## 5.2.2.12. Pharmacokinetic interaction studies

## Senaparib as victim drug

Senaparib and M9 mediated transport by P-gp, BCRP and OATP1B1/3 were investigated. Senaparib was also assessed as OAT1/3 and OCT2 substrate. Senaparib and M9 were found to be P-gp substrate in vitro.

Senaparib metabolism is mainly mediated by CYP3A4 which is responsible for the active major metabolite M9 formation. As all secondary metabolites of M9 were found in very low amounts in excreta and are postulated to be formed by different pathways, no further identification of the enzymes involved in the elimination of M9 is needed.

Effects of strong CYP3A4 inhibitor, such as itraconazole, or effect of strong inducer, such as rifampicin was assessed in the Clinical DDI study IMP4297-104 conducted in healthy Chinese volunteers. This study was a parallel fixed sequence two groups study where each group assessed senaparib DDI with itraconazole (N=16) and rifampicin (N=15) respectively.

In itraconazole group, under fasted state, subjects received 40 mg senaparib followed by a three-day wash-out period after which 200 mg QD itraconazole was administered for five days. The following day, 40 mg senaparib was administered under fasted state. One hour after senaparib administration a meal was administered followed by 200 mg itraconazole administration. For 3 subsequent days, subjects continued 200 mg itraconazole capsules alone QD. Senaparib, M9 and M14 were quantified by LC-MS/MS after senaparib administration.

In rifampicin group, under fasted state, subjects received 100 mg senaparib followed by a three-day wash-out period after which 600 mg QD rifampicin was administered for nine days. The following day, 100 mg senaparib was administered under fasted state. One hour after senaparib administration a meal was administered followed by 600 mg rifampicin administration. For 3 subsequent days, subjects continued 600 mg rifampicin alone QD.

The observed DDI study results are consistent with a CYP3A4 mediated metabolism.

The tested senaparib dose of 40 mg in the itraconazole group is likely within dose proportional dosing regimen. On the other hand, at the indicated dose of 100 mg senaparib, senaparib absorption is likely to be saturated. Senaparib interaction at 40 mg with itraconazole showed a 375% senaparib exposure (AUC0-inf) increase and a 79% Cmax increase.

In the presence of rifampicin, 83% senaparib exposure decrease, and 59% Cmax reduction were observed.

## Senaparib as perpetrator

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## In vitro results

At clinically relevant concentrations neither senaparib nor the major circulating metabolite M9 represent a risk of inhibition of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19 and CYP2D6. While M9 is not expected to be a clinically relevant CYP3A4 inhibitor, senaparib showed potential time-dependent inhibition in vitro on CYP3A4/5 at clinically relevant. Further determination the time-dependent inhibition parameters of this inhibition (kinact, the maximal inactivation rate constant and kI,u, the unbound inhibitor concentration causing half-maximal inactivation) was not performed. The clinical relevance of the observed CYP3A4 time dependent inhibition could not be excluded. Furthermore, it cannot be ruled out that senaparib is a direct CYP3A4 inhibitor at gut level.

Senaparib and M9 induction potential for CY1A2, 2B6 and 3A4 were evaluated in vitro by mRNA quantification following 48h exposure to the tested compounds. Senaparib was tested at 0.1, 1 and 10 µM whereas M9 was tested at 0.1, 0.33, 1, 3.3 and 10 µM. These studies results showed a mRNA concentration dependent increase exceeding the two-fold mRNA increase at concentration below 50 x Cmax,u (13.64 µM) in at least one donor. Therefore, both senaparib and M9 clinically relevant induction of CYP1A2, 2B6 and CYP3A4 substrate could not be excluded based on these results, nor a decrease of contraceptives effect. Furthermore, based on additional in vitro results, senaparib significant induction of CYP2C8, CYP2C9 and CYP2C19 could not be excluded in clinical settings. Except for CYP2C8, clinically relevant induction of CYP2C9 and CYP2C19 cannot be ruled out for M9 either.

In vitro, neither senaparib nor M9 inhibit OAT1, OAT3 and MATE2-K, and metabolite M9 does not inhibit P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B3, OCT1, OCT2 and MATE1 at clinically relevant concentrations. While there were no major concerns on the used test system for the DDI assessment mediated by transporter, the tested senaparib concentrations for P-gp, BCRP, OAPT1B1, OATP1B3 and OCT1 inhibitions were too low to exclude clinically relevant introductions due to solubility limitations. Nonetheless, the studies indicated that senaparib is likely to inhibit OCT2, MATE1, and M9 OATP1B1.

## 5.2.3. Pharmacodynamics

## 5.2.3.1. Mechanism of action

Senaparib capsule is a PARP inhibitor. When intracellular PARP is inhibited, a large number of singlestranded DNA damage inside will accumulate due to untimely repair, and unrepaired single-stranded DNA damage will trigger the disintegration of replication forks that reach here and thus produce a large number of double-stranded DNA damage. Such double-stranded DNA damage with a strong cytotoxicity can be repaired in normal cells in a timely and accurate manner by homologous recombination mediated by factors such as BRCA1 and BRCA2. In BRCA1/2-deficient cells, the probability of cell death is greatly increased due to the absence of the important pathway homologous recombination, resulting in inability to repair double-stranded DNA damage, or repair by less efficient non-homologous end joining

## 5.2.3.2. Primary and secondary pharmacology

No clinical pharmacology study evaluating the mode of action was provided. However, the mechanism of action of PARP inhibitors is known and 3 other PARPi are currently authorized in EU. Nevertheless, the range of exposure that are likely or not to have a therapeutic effect in patients was not assessed from a pharmacodynamics point of view. The dose response study IMP4297-2016-CN01 who was the basis for selecting the dose of 100 mg QD is described in below section 5.3.1.

A QT risk analysis was performed by the Applicant in 4 clinical studies conducted in healthy subjects who received a single dose of Senaparib 100mg.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.2.3.3. Pharmacodynamic interactions with other medicinal products or substances

N/A

## 5.2.3.4. Genetic differences in PD response

N/A

## 5.2.4. Pharmacokinetics/pharmacodynamics (PK/PD)

## Efficacy

A total of 128 patients with ovarian cancer from 3 studies were included in senaparib exposure-best overall response (BOR) analysis. Among them, the numbers (percentage) of patients assessed as Complete response (CR), Partial response (PR), Stable disease (SD) and Progressive disease (PD) were 7 (5.47%), 55 (43.0%), 50 (39.1%) and 16 (12.5%), respectively.

For all 128 patients with ovarian cancer, stacked histograms of the proportion of BOR by the initial dose (DOSE) and exposure measures (AVEDOSE, Cmaxss, Cminss and AUC24ss) are presented in Figure 14. and Figure 15, respectively. It showed that OR was achieved even at 20 mg QD, and the ORR was 52.8% at 100 mg QD. There was no positive trend between objective response rate (ORR) and exposure measures (AVEDOSE, Cmaxss, Cminss, AUC24ss). Boxplots of AVEDOSE and exposure measures by OR status are presented in Figure 16. It showed that the distributions of senaparib exposures in patients who achieved OR were comparable to those in patients who did not.

## Safety

Exposure-safety analyses were conducted using pooled data from the three clinical studies IMP4297-2016-AU01, IMP4297-2016-CN01 and IMP4297-2018-CN01. For total treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAE), 187 patients were evaluable; two subjects were excluded due to missing dates for their worst-grade TEAE. For all other analyses, including hematologic TEAE (TEAEH), treatment-related AEs (TRAE), serious AEs (SAE) and treatment-related SAEs (TR-SAE), 189 patients were included.

The Applicant explored relationship between steady-state exposure metrics (AUC24ss, Cmaxss, Cminss, AVEDOSE) and safety endpoints both graphically and by logistic regression modelling. Across all endpoints, similar trends were observed, with the incidence of high-grade adverse events increasing with dose and exposure and reaching an apparent plateau at doses equal to or above 80 mg once daily for ≥Grade 3 TEAE and TEAEH (Figure 18 and Table 19, Figure 19 and Table 20).

## 5.2.1. Dose selection and therapeutic window

According to the Applicant, the recommended clinical dose of senaparib (100 mg QD) was established based on the totality of PK, PD, E-R, and clinical data obtained across Phase I to Phase III studies.

## PK and dose-exposure relationship

Phase I studies (IMP4297-2016-AU01 and IMP4297-2016-CN01) evaluated senaparib at doses ranging from 2 to 150 mg QD in patients with advanced solid tumours. The Applicant reports that plasma exposure (AUC and Cmax) increased approximately proportionally up to around 80 mg, and then appeared to level off at higher doses (80-150 mg), suggesting saturation of absorption. Minimal accumulation was observed following repeated dosing, and the PK profile was considered compatible with QD administration. The PK were characterised by high IIV, mainly driven by variability in absorption. According to the Applicant, no dose-limiting toxicities (DLTs) occurred, and the maximum tolerated dose (MTD) was not reached. Overall, senaparib was reported to be well tolerated across the evaluated dose range, with AEs predominantly haematological and of mild to moderate intensity (Grade 1-2, NCI-CTCAE).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Exposure-response analyses

According to the Applicant, E-R analyses integrating data from Phase I and II studies indicated a relatively flat exposure-efficacy relationship, with the efficacy plateau reached within the exposure range corresponding to 100 mg QD. E-R models for safety suggested that the probability of ≥Grade 3 TEAEs increased with exposure but tended to stabilise between 80 mg and 100 mg QD. Model-based simulations indicated comparable safety profiles at these two dose levels.

Covariate analyses did not identify BW, race, or baseline renal or hepatic function as having a clinically relevant effect on senaparib exposure or the E-R relationships. The exposure range achieved at 100 mg QD was considered to lie within the therapeutic window derived from the E-R modelling results.

## Dose selection rationale

Based on the modelling and simulation outcomes, the Applicant considered 100 mg QD to provide exposures within the efficacy plateau and below the exposure threshold associated with unacceptable toxicity. Given the high IIV in systemic exposure, selection of the 100 mg QD regimen was intended to minimise the risk of sub-therapeutic exposure in part of the population while maintaining acceptable tolerability. The Applicant indicated that the observed exposure levels at 100 mg QD were consistent with those associated with maximal PARP inhibition in preclinical PD models.

In conclusion, according to the Applicant, the totality of PK, PD, and E-R modelling data support the selection of 100 mg QD as the recommended clinical dose for senaparib. This regimen was also the only one used in the pivotal Phase III study (IMP4297-301) for efficacy-safety evaluation, with dose modification (interruption, reduction, discontinuation) to be guided by individual tolerability in clinical practice.

## 5.2.2. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical pharmacology

## 5.2.2.1. Discussion

## Pharmacokinetics

The methods are considered adequate and comply with the acceptance criteria of the bioanalytical method validation of EMEA/CHMP/EWP/192217/2009 Rev. 1 Corr. 2** or of ICHM10 on bioanalytical method validation and study sample analysis.

## Food effect

A dedicated food-effect study with the modified senaparib capsule formulation showed a slight delay in absorption and a modest increase in overall exposure after a high-fat meal, which was deemed not clinically relevant. No new food-effect study was conducted for the commercial formulation due to its unchanged composition and demonstrated bioequivalence with the modified formulation under fasting conditions. Instead, an indirect comparison of two bioequivalence studies (10 mg vs 20 mg) under fed and fasted conditions suggested a potentially greater food effect (increase in exposure up to 82%) for the commercial formulation. Overall, due to the limited differences between formulations and the limitations of indirect comparison, it is considered unlikely that the food effect for the commercial formulation would differ significantly from that observed with the modified formulation. However, the SmPC recommendation for intake \"at least one hour before or two hours after the ingestion of food\", which is consistent with the practice in the pivotal trial, is acceptable as a conservative measure.

## Metabolite activity

The applicant was asked to estimate the contribution of M9 (and senaparib) to the pharmacological activity on PARPs, after accounting of their molecular mass, IC50, and unbound fraction, however the calculation was not provided. Based on the preceding, M9 contributes to 29% of the pharmacological activity on both PARPs.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Population-PK analysis and target population

The Pop-PK analysis for senaparib included 189 patients (2,427 PK observations) pooled from two Phase I studies (IMP4297-2016-AU01 and IMP4297-2016-CN01) and one Phase II study (IMP4297-2018-CN01) that tested doses from 2 to 150 mg QD. The final model was a two-compartment disposition model with sequential zero-/first-order absorption and first-order elimination. However, the unexplained variability (&gt;30%) and uncertainties are very high and shrinkage for absorption parameters (Ka and D1) is elevated in the final model, mainly driven by sparse PK sampling in the Phase II study. These points limit the reliability and predictive performance of the model.

Albumin and BW were identified as significant covariates on exposure. In particular, patients with low baseline albumin (~32 g/L) were predicted to have 1.5-fold higher AUC and nearly two-fold higher trough concentrations, suggesting a potential risk of increased systemic exposure. The Applicant acknowledged this increase; however, the clinical relevance remains uncertain due to the very small number of patients (n=10) and limitations of the Pop-PK and E-R analyses. No predefined dose adjustment is proposed, and dose individualisation based on tolerability (within the 60-100 mg QD range) is considered acceptable.

Since no PK data have been collected in the pivotal Phase III study IMP4297-301 and the main supportive Phase I and Phase II studies included in the Pop-PK analysis target different patient populations (subjects with advanced tumours or subjects with ovarian cancer carrying germline and/or somatic BRCA1/2 mutations versus subjects with FIGO Stage III-IV ovarian cancer in the phase III study) and have other clinical endpoints compared to the Phase III study, uncertainties remain on the exposure-response relationship for Selpercatinib in the phase III population, dose-adjustment in special populations and the dose chosen for the population to be treated. Overall, in the absence of E-R and a clear characterization of relevant PK metrics and related target ranges, the Pop-PK model is considered acceptable for descriptive purposes only (e.g. typical exposure and exploratory covariate effects), but not sufficiently robust to support dose justification or clinical decision-making.

## Impaired renal function

Results from the mass balance study showed that urinary excretion accounted for only 15% of the administered dose, indicating that renal elimination is a minor route of excretion. Nonetheless no dedicated study was conducted to assess the impact of renal impairment on senaparib PK and no data are available for patients with severe renal impairment or end-stage renal disease. The lack of data has been reflected in section 5.2 of the SmPC which also clarifies that no dosing recommendation can be provided for these patient subgroups. In addition, section 4.2 reflects appropriate warnings for use in these patients.

## Impaired hepatic function

No studies have been conducted to investigate the PKs of senaparib in hepatic impaired patients. Considering that hepatic impairment is likely to significantly alter the PK of the drug, especially since the main metabolism pathway is CYP-mediated (CYP3A4) and more than 40% of dose were recovered as metabolites, a dedicated study would allow dose adjustment to achieve comparable exposures across hepatic impairment degrees. The Applicant committed to conduct a dedicated hepatic impairment study post-authorisation (REC). The SmPC reflects the current uncertainties.

The Pop-PK analysis included mainly subjects with normal (87%) and mild (13%) hepatic function (NCIODWG criteria). No dose adjustment is needed in patients with mild hepatic impairment. Sepalna is not recommended to be used in patients with moderate (TB &gt;1.5 - 3.0 x ULN and any AST) and severe hepatic impairment (TB &gt;3 x ULN and any AST) as safety and pharmacokinetics have not been studied in these patients. It should only be used in patients with moderate and severe hepatic impairment if the potential benefit outweighs the risk. If use is considered necessary, patients should be carefully monitored (see sections 4.2 and 5.2 of the SmPC.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Effect of Body weight (BW) and race

Based on population PK analysis (PopPK), no clinically significant relationships were identified between predicted steady-state exposure and patient's race and body weight. Patients included in the population PK study were 80% Asian, 15% were White, and had body weights between 42 kg and 103 kg (median body weight was 63 kg). The consistent 10-15% difference in exposure between White and Asian patient populations observed in the clinical studies is judged not sufficiently significant for a dose-adjustment based on racial considerations. However, data in the non-Asian population are limited. The consistent 12-23% difference in exposure between patients weighing 85 kg and 110 kg patient populations observed in the clinical studies is judged not sufficiently significant for a dose-adjustment based on weight considerations. However, data on patients with extremely high body weight are limited.

## Gender

Based on the data collected in the Pop-PK analysis, a slight non-significant gender effect on CL/F is observed but given the limited data (males ~13.8%), no definite conclusion can be drawn. Of note, the male population to be treated will be very limited given that primary peritoneal cancer is very rare in men.

## Elderly

No dedicated study has been conducted in elderly subjects. Patients included in population PK (PopPK) analysis were aged 31 to 83 years (145 (76.7%) &lt;65 years, 35 (18.5%) 65-74 years, and 9 (4.8%) &gt;75-84 years). The PopPK analysis showed that the steady-state total exposures (AUC) of senaparib in patients aged 65-74 and patients aged 75-84 were 26% and 55% higher than that aged &lt;65. No dose adjustment is warranted for elderly patients aged 65 to 84. No data was available for patients aged 85 or over. The PK data in elderly patients remain limited but no dose adjustment is considered necessary in patients &lt;85 years, taking into consideration the availability of dose modification based on tolerability (see sections 4.2 and 5.2 of the SmPC).

## Exposure-response relationships and dose rationale

The E-R evaluations were based on pooled data from Phase I-II studies covering the 20-100 mg QD range (exploratory doses up to 150 mg QD were investigated in early phase I studies). No exposure-response analyses were conducted directly in the Phase III target population and the available E-R evaluations rely on Phase I/II data from patient groups whose comparability with the claimed population remains to be demonstrated. Consequently, the extrapolation of these results is uncertain.

Overall, the submitted E-R analyses suggest a flat exposure-efficacy relationship across the 20-100 mg range, with objective responses observed even at lower exposures and no discernible trend between systemic exposure and clinical endpoints (ORR, DOR, PFS). These findings suggest that near-maximal PD effect is achieved at doses below 100 mg. Conversely, safety analyses indicate a clear positive association between exposure and the incidence of ≥Grade 3 AEs, without clear evidence of a plateau beyond approximately 60-80 mg QD. The claimed flattening of the exposure-toxicity relationship appears to be largely influenced by extreme exposure values rather than a true saturation of toxicity. In clinical settings, frequent dose interruptions and reductions at 100 mg QD support the notion of exposure-driven toxicity.

In addition, systemic exposure does not increase proportionally beyond 80 mg QD, suggesting a possible saturation of absorption in the upper dose range. This introduces additional uncertainty when interpreting the apparent exposure-response plateau around 80 mg QD and limits the extent to which the E-R findings can be translated into a dose-response interpretation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Taken together, these findings indicate that the selection of 100 mg QD as the recommended regimen does not appear to have been guided by PK/PD optimisation, while the apparent saturation of absorption above 80 mg QD questions the relevance of increasing the dose beyond this level. Conversely, no robust E-R characterisation is available at intermediate doses (60 or 80 mg QD), precluding firm support for a lower dose. The 100 mg QD dose can therefore be accepted from a PK/PD perspective, as it is the only dose evaluated in the pivotal Phase III study and consequently the dose for which the clinical benefit-risk balance has been established. Nevertheless, its justification remains partly limited by the absence of direct E-R analyses in the Phase III target population and by the limited information available for intermediate doses. Therefore, the tolerability and safety profile at 100 mg QD should be thoroughly scrutinised and clearly demonstrated in support of the proposed regimen.

## PK interactions

## Effects of other medicinal products on senaparib

## Metabolism

Senaparib metabolism is mainly mediated by CYP3A4 which is responsible for the active metabolite M9 formation. To assess the magnitude of DDI of strong CYP3A4 or strong inducer on senaparib PK, the clinical DDI study IMP4297-104 has been conducted in healthy Chinese volunteers. This study was a parallel fixed sequence two groups study where each group assessed senaparib DDI with itraconazole (N=16) and rifampicin (N=15) respectively.

## Strong CYP3A4/P-gp inhibitor

At the dose of 100 mg of senaparib, absorption of senaparib is expected to reach saturation. Therefore, due to the lack of demonstration of senaparib PK linearity within 40 up to 100 mg, the interaction study results conducted at 40 mg could not be extrapolated to the interaction with itraconazole at the indicated dose of 100 mg senaparib. The conducted PK results of the clinical DDI study IMP4297-104 at 40 mg of senaparib with itraconazole suggest that a dose decrease down to 40 mg is likely to be insufficient. Indeed, when AUC0-inf observed after senaparib 40 mg + itraconazole is compared to senaparib 100 mg alone in the study IMP4297-104, a 1.8-fold increase is observed (combination group 1 geometric mean AUCinf itraconazole = 21300 ng.h/mL vs monotherapy group 2 geometric mean AUCinf 11700 ng.h/mL). While the pharmaceutical forms available allowed a dose decrease down to 20 mg, to maximize the patient's chances of achieving a therapeutic benefit, further quantitative investigations have been performed using pop PK model approach to further support this dose recommendation for patients who could not avoid senaparib associations with strong CYP3A4 inhibitor. The model used for this purpose had some limitations that precluded its use for the formal determination of the dosing regimen in clinical setting. However, in this case, the model is used only for descriptive purpose and covariate exploration (i.e. effect of strong CYP3A4 inhibitor).

Briefly, previous PoP PK model was used where PK data from the conducted DDI study IMP4297-104 with itraconazole were included. Covariate were re-evaluated based on the updated pooled dataset. The model predicted, in presence of strong CYP3A4 inhibitor, a 25 mg QD senaparib dose would be required to achieve senaparib AUCss following 100 mg senaparib QD administration.

Given the available pharmaceutical form of 20 mg, and the close match of simulated AUC,ss at 20 mg to AUC,ss at 100 mg senaparib, the proposed dose reduction down to 20 mg in case of concomitant treatment with strong CYP3A4 inhibitor is supported by the CHMP.

## Moderate CYP3A4/P-gp inhibitor

In case of concomitant treatment with moderate CYP3A4/P-gp inhibitors, the appropriate dose of senaparib remains undefined, despite the availability of PK results from a clinical DDI study with strong CYP3A4/P-gp inhibitors. No further investigation of the impact of the concomitant treatment with moderate CYP3A4 / P-gp inhibitors on senaparib PK has been planned by the Applicant. However, the Applicant has empirically proposed to reduce senaparib dose to 40 mg. Considering the senaparib dose reduction to 20 mg once daily (QD) when co-administration with a strong CYP3A4/P-gp inhibitor cannot be avoided and the available commercial formulation available (senaparib capsules, 20 mg), this proposed dose reduction with appropriate monitoring for adverse reactions seems to be the most suitable approach in case of unavoidable coadministration in the target population (e.g., in cases of fungal or bacterial infections). However, given the uncertainties and as a cautionary measure, subsequent dose reduction down to 20mg QD is recommended based on tolerability.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Weak CYP3A4/P-gp inhibitor

No data was provided to support dose adjustment in case of concomitant treatment with weak CYP3A4/P-gp inhibitor. However, the senaparib exposure increase is expected to be mild. Caution of use has been reported in section 4.5 of the SmPC with subsequent dose reduction depending on the drug tolerability.

## Strong and Moderate inducer

Strong inducer senaparib association with strong or moderate inhibitor is not recommended based on the clinical DDI study conducted with rifampicin where a 83% decrease in AUC was observed. This significant decrease may significantly reduce senaparib efficacy. This has been reported in SmPC.

## Weak CYP inducer

Efficacy analyses indicated a flat E-R, with objective responses observed even at lower exposures and OR was achieved even at 20 mg QD. Therefore, the impact of weak inducer on senaparib efficacy is expected to be low, and no adjustment can be considered needed.

## Drug transporters

Senaparib is both a substrate of CYP3A4 and P-gp. Although a significant interaction of itraconazole on senaparib PK was observed, as itraconazole is both P-gp and CYP3A4 substrate, P-gp contribution in senaparib elimination could not be discriminated from CYP3A4 metabolism. Only combined restrictions with CYP3A4/P-gp inhibitors are provided in the SmPC.

## Acid reducing Agents (ARA)

A decrease of 40-fold in the solubility of senaparib between pH 1.2 and higher pH is observed. Based on a comparative analysis of pivotal Phase III study IMP4297-301 results, no clinically meaningful impact on efficacy or safety were found in patients treated by senaparib with ARA vs. patients treated by senaparib without ARA. Furthermore, senaparib is classified as BCS class IV with a flat exposure-efficacy relationship across the 20-100 mg range. Therefore, the risk of a clinically relevant PK DDI with acid-reducing agents is expected to be low.

## Effects of senaparib on other medicinal products

## CYP3A4 substrates

Senaparib net effect on CYP3A4 drug substrate PK is considered unknown given its time-dependent inhibition and induction of CY3A4. Furthermore, it cannot be ruled out that senaparib may show a direct inhibition potential of CYP3A4 at clinically relevant concentrations in the gastro-intestinal tract since the concentrations of senaparib investigated in the direct CYP3A4 inhibition in vitro experiment do not cover the worst-case scenario.

It may be difficult to some patients to avoid this association as most of drugs are CYP3A4 drug substrates. The Applicant agreed to conduct a clinical DDI study with midazolam (REC) as probe substrate to determine the clinical effect of senaparib on CYP3A4 substrates. In the meantime, SmPC advises caution of use when these association could not be avoided with both safety and efficacy monitoring of the CYP3A4 drug substrates.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## CYP1A2, CY2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19 substrates

Presently no clinical data are available to assess the clinical relevance of senaparib induction of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19 in vitro. Given this high interacting drug profile (all mandatory CYPs isoforms besides CYP2D6), the Applicant is requested to perform a cocktail study to further assess the clinical relevance of the in vitro DDI studies findings, notably senaparib induction potentials on CYP1A2, CY2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19. (REC)

Caution of use in case of concomitant treatment with drug substrates of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP3A4 is recommended, and reflected in SmPC and PIL especially on the effect on the drug substrates that are with narrow therapeutic range.

## Drug-drug interaction with hormonal contraceptives

The Applicant has provided adequate warning on senaparib potential to reduce hormonal oral contraceptives due to its induction potential.

## Transporter substrates

## P-gp drug substrates

The clinically relevant effect on P-gp transporters cannot be ruled out. In vitro results showed both inhibitory and induction potential of P-gp by senaparib. The net effect of senaparib on P-gp drug substrates is unknown.

## BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT1, OCT2 and MATE1 substrates

The clinically relevant inhibition effect on BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT1, OCT2 and MATE1 transporters by senaparib cannot be excluded based on in vitro results, as well as on OATP1B1 by M9 which is reflected in SmPC 4.5 and 5.2 sections.

In particular, the magnitude of PK interactions in clinical settings has not been documented for commonly prescribed statins (OATP1B1/3 substrates). Given that the Applicant has agreed to conduct a clinical DDI study with midazolam, it is recommended that an endogenous biomarker approach be integrated to evaluate the inhibition of hepatic OATP1B by senaparib. Specifically, the measurement of plasma coproporphyrin I should be included (REC).

## 5.2.2.2. Conclusions

Overall, the clinical PK of senaparib and its main metabolite M9 has been adequately characterized.

From a drug-drug interaction perspective, senaparib was shown to be a highly interacting drug. Clinically relevant interaction with CYP1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19 and 3A4 have been reflected in SmPC and further clinical DDI studies as recommended by the CHMP (RECs) will be provided to further characterise senaparib drug-drug interaction potentials with CYP3A4, and OATP1B drug substrates respectively including a clinical cocktail study to assess the clinical relevance of the in vitro induction findings on CYP1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9 and 2C19.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.3. Clinical efficacy

## 5.3.1. Dose response studies

IMP4297-2016-CN01 (hereinafter referred to as the Study CN01) is a multicenter, open-label Phase I study conducted in China targeting subjects with advanced solid tumours. This study includes doseescalation and dose-expansion phases, aiming to evaluate the safety, tolerability, PK characteristics, and efficacy of senaparib in humans, and to determine the MTD and/or RP2D.

## Study Design

Dose escalation stage. Participants in each group first received a single dose to observe its safety and PK characteristics, then entered the continuous dosing phase.

Dose expansion phase (dose groups where ≥1 subject achieved CR or PR according to RECIST v1.1 criteria or demonstrated significant reduction in tumour markers). Following the priority of dose escalation, approximately 10 additional subjects (approximately 20 subjects in the RP2D group) with advanced solid tumours positive for BRCA gene mutations (excluding ovarian cancer, fallopian tube cancer, and primary peritoneal cancer) were enrolled in each group.

## Study population

Histologically or cytologically confirmed unresectable advanced solid tumours that have failed at least one line of systemic therapy; In addition, positive BRCA gene mutation (except ovarian carcinoma, fallopian tube carcinoma and primary peritoneal carcinoma) is required during dose expansion. Patients with advanced breast carcinoma, ovarian carcinoma, fallopian tube carcinoma, primary peritoneal carcinoma and prostate carcinoma were preferred.

## Study Endpoints

Primary objective: To evaluate the safety and tolerability of single and multiple doses of senaparib capsule in patients with advanced solid tumours to determine the maximum tolerated dose and/or the recommended dose for phase II clinical trials.

Exploratory efficacy endpoints: ORR, DOR, DCR, PFS

## Results

## Subject Disposition

From August 30, 2017 (study start date) to June 9, 2020 (data cutoff date), a total of 57 subjects were screened and qualified to enter the study (33 subjects enrolled in the dose escalation phase and 24 subjects enrolled in the dose expansion phase).

Nine subjects (15.8%) were still participating in the study, and 48 subjects (84.2%) completed the study. Reasons for study completion include evidence of PD in 30 subjects (52.6%), subjects themselves or their primary physicians requested to stop study treatment for other reasons in 13 subjects (22.8%), intolerable toxicity or cumulative toxicity leading to inability to continue medication in 3 subjects (5.3%), and death in 2 subjects (3.5%).

## Demographics and Baseline Characteristics

Among the 57 subjects enrolled in the CN01 study, the median age was 54.0 years (33-68 years). Females accounted for 48 subjects (84.2%), and the vast majority (55 subjects, 96.5%) were of Han ethnicity. The median weight of all subjects was 62.20 kg (45.0-93.7 kg), and the median BMI was 23.70 kg/m2 (16.5-34.8 kg/m2); ECOG performance status scores were all 0 or 1.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Among the 57 subjects enrolled in the CN01 study, 32 (56.1%) tested positive for the BRCA gene. The majority of tumour staging was stage IV (53 subjects, 93%); 35 subjects (61.4%) had previously received ≥3 lines of systemic therapy, and 22 subjects (38.6%) had received 1-2 lines of systemic therapy. There were 28 (49.1%) ovarian cancer subjects.

## Efficacy and Safety Results

All 57 subjects enrolled in this study and treated with senaparib were included in the ITT population, including 32 subjects with BRCA mutations. Of these, 44 subjects had target lesions at baseline and had at least one post-treatment imaging assessment; 26 BRCA mutation subjects had target lesions at baseline, had at least one post-treatment imaging assessment. The PFS analysis was based on evaluations of all 57 subjects in the ITT population and all 32 BRCA mutation subjects; ORR and DCR analyses were based on the aforementioned 44 and 26 subjects; the DOR analysis was conducted based on the 10 subjects who achieved PR and among them, the 7 BRCA mutation subjects.
