## Study Objectives and Endpoints

Primary objectives of this study are:

- ⚫ To evaluate the safety and tolerability of single and multiple doses of IMP4297 administered to patients with advanced solid tumours
- ⚫ To determine the MTD and evaluate the dose limiting toxicities (DLTs) of IMP4297

Exploratory efficacy endpoints: ORR, DCR, DOR, PFS

## Study Population

## Subject Disposition

From January 10, 2017 (first observation date) to April 15, 2020 (data cutoff date), a total of 39 subjects were enrolled, all of whom received senaparib treatment. The dosage levels included 2 mg (1 subject), 6 mg (3 subjects), 10 mg (3 subjects), 20 mg (5 subjects), 30 mg (5 subjects), 40 mg (4 subjects), 80 mg (4 subjects), 100 mg (6 subjects), 120 mg (5 subjects), and 150 mg (3 subjects) QD.

Seven subjects (17.9%) are still participating in the study, and 32 subjects (82.1%) have completed the study. Reasons for termination include disease progression in 19 subjects (48.7%); withdrawal decided by physician in 7 subjects (17.9%); subject withdrawal in 3 subjects (7.7%); adverse events in 1 subject (2.6%); and other reasons in 2 subjects (5.1%).

## Demographics and Baseline Characteristics

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

As of April 15, 2020, among the 39 subjects enrolled in the AU01 study, the median age was 70.0 years (42-83 years). Females accounted for 22 subjects (56.4%), and the majority were white (35 subjects, 89.7%). The median weight of all subjects was 71.90 kg (45.4-103.1 kg), and the median BMI was 25.670 kg/m2 (17.51-36.59 kg/m2); baseline ECOG performance scores were all 0 or 1.

Among the 39 subjects enrolled in the AU01 study, 8 subjects (23.5%) tested positive for the BRCA gene. The majority were at stage IV advanced solid tumour (30 subjects, 90.9%). The mean (standard deviation) disease course of advanced tumour was 45.1 months (45.84 months). Fifteen subjects had previously received ≥3 lines of systemic therapy, 10 subjects (31.3%) had received 2 lines of therapy, and 6 subjects (18.8%) had received 1 line of therapy. There were 11 subjects (28.2%) with ovarian cancer.

## Efficacy Results

All 39 subjects enrolled in this study and treated with senaparib were included in the efficacy population. Among them, 22 subjects had target disease at baseline and received at least one postbaseline tumour assessment. The following efficacy results are based on the evaluation of these 22 subjects.

Based on RECIST v1.1, the investigator-assessed Best Overall Response (BOR) included Stable Disease (SD) in 68.2% (15/22), Progressive Disease (PD) in 18.2% (4/22), and Partial Response (PR) in 13.6% (3/22). No subjects had a result of Complete Response (CR) or Not Evaluable (NE). The overall Disease Control Rate (DCR) was 81.8% (18/22) for those who had an SD assessment at least 6 weeks after baseline. Among the 8 subjects carrying BRCA mutations, the DCR was 83.3% (5/8, 95% CI: 35.9, 99.6). In the 20 mg, 100 mg, and 120 mg groups of this study, each had one subject who achieved PR, survived, and did not experience disease progression; among them, two subjects were BRCA mutation. The Duration of Response (DOR) for these three subjects was 22.11 months, 2.76 months, and 1.41 months, respectively. The median Progression-Free Survival (PFS) for all 22 subjects was 5.72 months (95% CI: 2.66, 7.36).

In the 100 mg group, one subject had a BOR of PR; one subject had a BOR of SD

## 5.3.6. Analysis performed across trials (pooled analyses and metaanalysis)

Not applicable

## 5.3.7. Healthcare professional engagement

Input received from healthcare professional organisation in the context of CHMP early dialogue:

Ovarian cancer is a frequent disease and the fifth leading cause of death from cancer in women. Due to the lack of early symptoms of disease and the failure to establish useful screening, most women are diagnosed at advanced stage. Initial treatment consists of maximal surgery (\"optimal debulking\"), before systemic treatment, after systemic treatment (\"interval debulking\"), or both. Systemic treatment mainly consists of platinum and taxane chemotherapy, with the only advanced in the last several years being bevacizumab (with or after chemotherapy) and PARP inhibitor maintenance treatment.

Recurrence of disease when more than six months have passed after response to initial treatment is considered \"platinum sensitive\" and is subject to the same or similar systemic treatment. First or subsequent recurrence of disease when less than six months have passed after response to initial treatment is considered \"platinum resistant\" and is subject to treatment with various chemotherapeutic agents, most commonly as monotherapy.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Prognosis is poor and quality of life is low during the \"platinum resistant\" phase of the disease. As such, any treatment that can delay recurrence or improve on the results of available chemotherapy is bound to be useful. Even if the benefits in the maintenance setting focus on extending the recurrence/progression-free interval, given the duration of this treatment and the fact that the alternative would be no treatment at all, toxicity should be assessed against potential overall survival benefits, and not only postponement of recurrence. Overall survival remains the most important endpoint and most clinical trials should be able to measure it as co-primary or key secondary endpoint without undue delay in the readout of primary results.

## 5.3.8. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical efficacy

## 5.3.8.1. Discussion

The clinical development plan for senaparib in the sought indication \"maintenance treatment of adult patients with advanced epithelial high-grade ovarian, fallopian tube or primary peritoneal cancer who are in response following completion of first-line platinum-based chemotherapy\" includes one single randomised placebo controlled phase 3 pivotal study (IMP4297-301) supported by one phase II study (IMP4297-2018-CN01) in patients with advanced ovarian cancer with BRCA mutation in ≥ third line setting, and 2 dose escalation phase I studies (IMP4297-2016-CN01 and IMP4297-2016-AU01) in advanced solid tumours with safety and tolerability as primary objectives. The supportive studies are of limited value to support efficacy in the maintenance setting.

All but one studies were conducted in China only, and the IMP4297-2016-AU01 study was conducted in Australia. No CHMP Scientific Advice was sought for the applied indication.

## Dose response study

No dedicated phase II dose-response study was conducted to support the selection of the intended posology. The choice of the 100mg QD dosing was made based on one dose escalation/dose expansion phase I study in patients with advanced solid tumours (IMP4297-2016-CN01). The primary endpoint was safety, and exploratory endpoints included ORR, DOR, DCR and PFS. Efficacy was thus assessed as a response to a progressive disease in patients who have failed at least one prior line of systemic therapy. In the dose expansion phase, all patients were required to have a positive BRCA gene mutation. Thus, the dose was not chosen based on optimal data, i.e. in a different setting and different tumour types.

No DLT was observed at any dose level, and tumour responses were observed at 20, 60, 80, and 100 mg QD (No CR). The ORR, including all histology's and doses, was 22.7% (11.8% at the 100 mg dose). In the 26 BRCAm subjects, ORR was 26.9% including the 60 mg, 80mg and 100 mg doses (18.2% at the 100 mg dose).

Considering that no clear correlation between dose and efficacy was observed (See Discussion on Clinical Pharmacology) and the exploratory activity data and safety data from study CN01, with unclear responses at the intended dose in the target population, the choice of the 100 mg over the 80 mg dose is not fully justified. However, as the single pivotal phase III study was conducted with the 100 mg dose, no efficacy data are available with a lower dose level, the benefit will be determined with the available data.

## Main study

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A single pivotal study (IMP4297-301) was provided to support the sought indication. This was a phase 3 randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled study conducted in China to evaluate the efficacy and safety of senaparib maintenance monotherapy following first-line platinum-based chemotherapy in patients with FIGO stage III-IV ovarian cancer.

Randomisation was performed using a 2:1 ratio to senaparib or placebo. The randomisation stratification factors (Response to first-line platinum-based chemotherapy and Baseline BRCA status) are deemed appropriate. Other stratification factors could have been considered (e.g. HRD status, or neoadjuvant chemotherapy use), but subgroup analyses have been performed on these baseline characteristics that should allow some consistency assessment.

The submission of one single pivotal study can be acceptable in accordance with the Guideline on the clinical evaluation of anticancer medicinal products (EMA/CHMP/205/95 Rev.6). Given that pivotal studies of other PARP inhibitors have demonstrated a positive benefit-risk balance in the same patient population, and that this is not an area characterised by previous failed studies, the proposed strategy is considered acceptable. However, reliance on a single pivotal study requires the evidence of efficacy to be sufficiently compelling in term of internal and external validity, clinical relevance, statistical significance, data quality, and internal consistency (CHMP/EWP/2330/99).

In Europe, the currently approved first line maintenance treatments include PARP inhibitors (olaparib approved in EU for BRCAm/HRD+ patients, niraparib and rucaparib for all patients with CR/PR after first-line platinum-based chemotherapy, regardless of the BRCAm status) and bevacizumab for frontline treatment (combination) and subsequent maintenance therapy regardless of BRCAm status. The applicant justified the choice of placebo instead of a PARPi as a comparator with the fact that the study started before any PARPi was approved for first line maintenance therapy of ovarian cancer regardless of BRCA status which is acknowledged (first subject signed ICF in Nov 2019). However, although olaparib, in the first line maintenance of patients with BRCA mutation, was not yet authorised in China, it was already authorised in EU in May 2019 It is however difficult to evaluate the uptake of olaparib after the first MA for patients with BRCAm; nevertheless, for HRP patient with high risk of recurrence, bevacizumab could have been considered as a comparator. At the time of study initiation (2019), bevacizumab maintenance treatment was not approved in China, but was already approved and recommended for HRD-negative FIGO III/IV ovarian cancer patients following complete or partial response to platinum+ paclitaxel first-line chemotherapy in the EU.

According to the ESMO guideline, for the FIGO III/IV ovarian cancer patients who achieved CR/PR after no prior bevacizumab in their first-line therapy (only platinum-based chemotherapy), bevacizumab monotherapy is still standard of care for HRD-negative patient subgroup (alternatively, bevacizumab is at least considered as a treatment option according to EU local practice). Therefore, placebo is not considered suitable as comparator at least for HRD-negative FIGO III/IV ovarian cancer patients.

Finally, it is noted that all three currently authorised PARP inhibitors in 1 st line maintenance treatment of stage III/IV ovarian cancer, whether in patients with BRCAm or in all comer population, were evaluated against placebo.

Having considered all the above, the use of a placebo control will not be further challenged.

In study 301, patients received senaparib 100 mg QD or matching placebo for 2 years or until PD, whichever occurs earlier. Subjects with evidence of stable disease at 2 years could continue to receive the treatment if the investigator assesses that the benefits outweigh the risks of continuing the treatment from a benefit-risk perspective. However, data from the patients who received senaparib for more than 2 years were scarce. Treatment duration proposed in section 4.2 of the SmPC to continue treatment until radiological disease progression, unacceptable toxicity or completion of 2 years treatment is therefore acceptable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Patients with newly diagnosed FIGO stage III-IV high grade serous, high grade endometrioid or other histological types of epithelial ovarian cancer, fallopian tube cancer or primary peritoneal cancer carrying BRCA mutation were eligible and must have reached CR or PR after completion of platinumbased chemotherapy as per RECISTv1.1 in the first line. Subjects could not use vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF)/vascular endothelial growth factor receptor (VEGFR) inhibitors and/or immune checkpoint inhibitors in combination or maintenance treatment during the course of first-line therapy. The proposed indication is for \"all-comers\" population, regardless any biomarker status. BRCA mutation status was centrally tested at baseline for stratification. The results for BRCA mutation testing were considered for inclusion of patients with other histological types only, and not for patients with high grade serous or endometrioid tumours.

External validity (CHMP/EWP/2330/99) was questioned during the assessment. The applicant provided a comparison of epidemiology, clinical diagnosis and treatment of ovarian cancer in China and in Europe. Overall, while the incidence, mortality and 5-year prevalence are twice higher in European patients than in Chinese patients, it is agreed that the repartition of pathology type, staging, BRCA and HRD, can be considered similar. The applicant further displayed clinical practice for diagnosis and treatment for ovarian cancer (symptoms, diagnosis, surgery, systemic therapy for newly diagnosed OC) which can be considered similar in both regions.

Population Objectives and endpoints

The primary objective of study 301 was to assess efficacy with PFS by BICR based on RECIST v1.1. as primary endpoint. The ITT analysis population used for primary efficacy analyses included all randomised patients, which is appropriate. The primary analysis methods themselves (KM method, log-rank test and Cox model stratified by the randomisation stratification factors) are standard and acceptable.

PFS assessed by BICR as primary endpoint is acceptable when OS is evaluated as secondary (EMA/CHMP/205/95 Rev.6). In this setting, in which patients may receive multiple subsequent treatment lines, which could hamper the analysis of OS, it is considered acceptable. OS is one of the secondary endpoints. In addition, other secondary endpoint such as PFS2 or TFST are considered clinically relevant, although PFS2 is considered of marginal relevance since patients were not followed after progression according to the censoring rules.

Although the study was double-blinded, there is a risk of functional unblinding due to the marked safety profile of senaparib. In this context, the BICR assessment should help reduce the potential bias that might be more associated with investigator assessment.

The GCP inspection also revealed that 12.5% of participants were unblinded at the investigator's request, mostly following disease progression, a process not described in the protocol or the CSR. In response, the applicant provided a CSR addendum detailing the rationale and timing of each instance, together with a worst-case sensitivity analysis addressing the 5 patients unblinded prior to BICRconfirmed progression. Results were consistent with the primary analysis, providing sufficient reassurance regarding the robustness of the PFS results.

The primary estimand of the PFS analysis (which addresses ICEs of new anti-cancer treatments) is not aligned with EMA guidance. Indeed, a treatment policy strategy would have been preferred to address all ICEs (EMA/CHMP/27994/2008/Rev.1). It is acknowledged that a post-hoc analysis has been performed by the Applicant based on a supplementary estimand that is more aligned with EMA

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

guidance. However, per protocol, subjects continued to receive tumour assessment only until PD or initiation of subsequent anti-tumour treatment. Therefore, this supplementary estimand is not supported by study data and the corresponding analysis is of limited value.

The primary PFS analysis censored participants with ≥2 missing measurements before PD, which is not aligned with EMA guidance. It also assumes non-informative censoring, but this assumption may not hold true for participants who are non-administratively censored (i.e. patients censored for reasons other than complete follow-up at database cut-off). The hypothetical strategy applied to the initiation of new anti-cancer treatment for the primary estimand has contributed to the amount of nonadministrative censoring. Nevertheless, a tipping point sensitivity analysis was performed and supported the robustness of the primary PFS results to departures from this assumption.

Secondary endpoints included OS, PFS and PFS2 by investigator, CFI, TFST TDT, and TOI of FACT-O.

It should be noted that all endpoints other than the primary PFS and secondary OS endpoints are not controlled for type I error and should therefore be considered of exploratory nature. The applicant did not make use of the estimand framework for secondary endpoints other than OS, which makes the target treatment effect unclear. Accordingly, these endpoints are considered supportive and should be interpreted with caution.

The multiplicity adjustment of OS and its interim analyses were only introduced in the SAP, as they were not included in the protocol. Acknowledging the double-blind nature of the trial and more importantly the non-significance of OS results, the issue is not thought to impact study conclusions.

## Conduct of the study

Study 301 (FLAMES, IMP4297-301) was conducted only in China (41 sites). The first subject sign ICF was November 27, 2019 and the study is still ongoing, with a DCO March 16 2023 for the primary analysis (PFS), and a DCO of May 17, 2024, for the OS analysis.

The protocol of study 301 was amended once 2 years after the start of the study, notably to include patients with high grade endometrioid carcinoma since they can benefit from PARPi. In addition, an exploratory objective to assess the relationship between HRD and efficacy was also introduced. The latter implies that only patients included after the implementation of this amendment were assessed for HRD status. This is further discussed below.

Less than 10% of subjects had an important protocol deviation. It is agreed that the most frequent important protocol deviations (\"no safety assessment after last dose\", \"stratification error\", \"receipt of another treatment than protocol specified\" and \"ineligibility according to inclusion criteria\") were balanced between both treatment groups, although they were systematically higher in senaparib group. It is also agreed that approximately 40% of the important protocol deviations in senaparib group were related to safety assessment, and that a sensitivity analysis was conducted according to actual stratification factors. Nevertheless, other major important deviations could have impacted the results (i.e. selection criteria, concomitant medications, treatment interruptions). According to the CSR of study 301, subjects who have no major protocol deviations affecting efficacy assessments were included in the Per protocol analysis. However, the PPS population includes 131/133 patients in placebo group and 269/271 patients in senaparib group when it appears that more patients should have been excluded of the PPS population according to the above cited major protocol deviations. It was clarified that the criteria to exclude patients from the PPS population were major protocol deviations considered to have an effect on efficacy.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Overall, 588 patients were screened, and 404 patients were successfully included and randomized 2:1 in senaparib group (n=271) or in placebo group (n=133), thus, 30% of patients failed screening. The main reasons for screen failure were failure to meet inclusion criteria \"Subjects who have completed protocol required first-line platinum-based chemotherapy and achieved CR/PR\" (49 patients), \"have normal organ and bone marrow function\" (38 patients), and exclusion criteria \"Subjects with Myelodysplastic Syndrome (MDS)/Acute Myeloid Leukaemia (AML) (19 patients)\".

At the DCO 16 March 2023, 73/271 patients in the senaparib group and 17/133 patients in the placebo group still received the treatment. The main reason for discontinuation in both treatment arms was disease progression (n=83/271 and 81/133 in senaparib and placebo arm respectively) followed by end of planned treatment for 2 years (n=68/271 and 22/133 respectively), and subject decision (n=27/271 and 11/133 respectively). In senaparib arm, 12 patients discontinued study treatment due to intolerable adverse events (vs none in placebo arm). 234 patients in senaparib and 118 patients in placebo arm were in follow up at the DCO.

Overall, the procedures for testing and selection of patients for this pivotal trial remain unclear and the detailed local testing methods for testing of germline and/or somatic BRCA1/2 mutations, as used during the pre-screening, was not collected in the Case Report Form and not mentioned in the CSR. Therefore, the tests used for local testing are not known/reported. For the central testing of BRCA1/2 mutations, it was based on next-generation sequencing (NGS) using the Illumina platform by AmoyDx.

## Demographic characteristics

In general, the provided baseline characteristics are similar between 2 arms.

In study 301, Mean age of participants were similar in both treatment arms with 55.4 years (SD=8.63) and 55.4 years (SD=8.67) in placebo et senaparib arms respectively, with more than the half between 50 and 65 years. The vast majority (&gt; 95%) was of Han ethnicity in both treatment arms, and all the patients were Asian. Mean height and weight were rather inferior to the European means and to what has been observed in prior multinational phase III clinical studies of PARPi registration including European patients. However, the popPK analysis investigating the effect of body weight suggests that the difference in systemic exposure to senaparib across the body weight spectrum remain modest and are not clinically relevant.

In both treatment groups, patients with ECOG 0 and I were represented although a majority of patients had ECOG 1 in both placebo (57.9%) and senaparib arm (60.9%). There were more subjects who had prior blood transfusion in senaparib arm 16 patients (6%) than in placebo arm (3.7%). In particular, 9 patients in senaparib arm had bone marrow depression while none in placebo arm.

Most of the patients in both treatment arms had CR following first line chemotherapy (89.5% in placebo and 87.5% in senaparib arm). This is higher than in previous pivotal studies for other authorised PARPi in the same setting, which could partially explain the higher mPFS observed in FLAMES than in PRIMA, ATHENA or SOLO1 studies.

Tumour diagnosis and staging were similar in both treatment arms, with, however, more patients with a stage IV disease in senaparib arm (31.0%) than in placebo arm (26.3%). History of treatment with anti-tumour drug and history of tumour surgeries were overall balanced between both treatment arms (primary/interval cytoreductive surgery), but with however, less gross residual disease after debulking surgery in the senaparib arm. However, subgroup analysis and post-hoc analyses of PFS and OS adjusted for the gross residual disease after debulking surgery at baseline did not suggest that the imbalance gross residual disease after debulking surgery at baseline had an impact on the clinical outcomes.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Mutation status

In the pivotal study FLAMES, approximately one-third of patients in each treatment arm had a BRCA1/2-mutated tumour at baseline, predominantly pathogenic BRCA1 mutations. Although this prevalence was higher than generally reported in ovarian cancer, it was balanced across treatment arms and consistent with other pivotal first-line maintenance studies, likely reflecting enrichment through platinum sensitivity. HRD status was available for 222 out of 271 patients with available tumour tissue sample and was analysed retrospectively to support exploratory analyses. HRD positivity with or without BRCA1/2-mutation is a predictive biomarker of response to PARPis. Generally, baseline characteristics for subgroups by BRCA1/BRCA2 and homologous recombination deficiency status were balanced between the 2 arms.

## Results

## Primary outcome: PFS by BICR

At the primary analysis (DCO Mar 16, 2023), Study 301 met its primary endpoint demonstrating a significantly longer mPFS in senaparib arm than in placebo arm. At the interim analysis (when 170 BICR PFS events occurred), the median follow-up durations for the senaparib group and the placebo group were 22.37 months (0.1-38.2 months) and 22.18 months (4.2-35.4 months), respectively. A statistically significant improvement in PFS by BICR was shown in the senaparib group compared with the placebo group: The median PFS in placebo arm was 13.6 months (95% CI: 10.9, 16.6) and not reached (NR) in senaparib arm, p&lt;0.0001 (stratified HR of 0.43, 95% CI 0.32, 0.58). The KM curves start to separate after the first tumour assessment (3 months) and remains apart until the data cut-off (16 March 2023). The PFS survival rate at 12 months was 53.69% (95% CI 44.37, 62.11) in placebo arm and 72.15% (95% CI 66.09, 77.31) in senaparib arm. At 24 months, the PFS survival rate was 31.31% (95% CI 22.57, 40.42) in placebo arm and 63.01% (95% CI 56.27, 69.00) in senaparib arm.

As per the guideline (CPMP/EWP/2330/99) prerequisite for one pivotal study, is that it must be exceptionally compelling, and it is agreed that the treatment effect was overall consistently in favour of senaparib arm across centres in terms of PFS.

Sensitivity analyses by investigator assessed PFS, using actual stratification factors, PPS population, censoring rules, were overall consistent with the primary analysis although median PFS was reached in senaparib group in this latter sensitivity analysis. An alternative sensitivity analysis was requested using EMA censoring rules. However, since subjects were to continue to receive tumour assessment until PD or initiation of subsequent anti-tumour treatment, tumour assessments are expected to be missing after the initiation of new anti-tumour treatment.

Overall, the results of the primary endpoint at the interim analysis showed a clinically relevant benefit of senaparib over placebo as maintenance therapy in patients with high grade serous ovarian cancer with CR or PR after a first line of chemotherapy. This finding is consistent with the benefit observed for other PARPi in the same population.

Nevertheless, the control arm (placebo) appears to perform better than for previously reported studies in the similar setting. Indeed, when considering data from other PARPi in the same setting, the median PFS in placebo arm was 9.2 months (95% CI 8.3-12.2) for Rubraca and 8.2 months (95% CI 7.3, 8.5) for Zejula. This might be related to the higher incidence of patients with a CR following prior platinumbased therapy than in other pivotal studies for PARPi in the same setting.

## Secondary outcomes

OS data were immature at the cut-off date 16 March 2023. At the second planned interim analysis (around 100 OS events, DCO 17 May 2024), mOS was still not reached in both treatment arms. The median follow-up was then 33 months. The OS maturity increased from approximately 10% to 24.8%, and then a favourable trend on OS was reported. No detrimental effect is suggested on OS from this interim analysis. The applicant will submit the final OS data as a Post authorisation efficacy study (PAES) in Annex II.D by 30 June 2027. These data are considered key to the B/R balance and will address criteria 2a of the Commission Delegated Regulation (EU) No. 357/2014 (when an initial efficacy assessment that is based on surrogate endpoints, which requires verification of the impact of the intervention on clinical outcome or disease progression or confirmation of previous efficacy assumptions).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Investigator-Assessed PFS has been evaluated as part as the sensitivity analyses of the primary analysis. Investigator-assessed mPFS was 11.1 months (95% CI 9.4, 15.5) in placebo arm and not reached (95% CI 26.251, NA) in senaparib arm. Therefore, although the mPFS was shorter when investigator assessed compared to BICR assessed, the mPFS effect size can be considered of high magnitude.

The applicant has provided an estimation of the overall consistency rate between the BICR and the investigator assessment results (87.9%), and a kappa statistic of 0.756, supporting an adequate level of agreement.

The median chemotherapy free interval was not reached in senaparib arm and was 16.7 months on placebo group. HR was 0.41 (95% CI: 0.30, 0.55), suggesting a benefit also in CFI. Median Time to First Subsequent Anti-Cancer Therapy or Death (TFST) was not reached in senaparib arm, and was 14.39 months in placebo arm. HR was 0.44 (95% CI: 0.33, 0.59). This suggests that compared with placebo, senaparib can significantly prolong the TFST of subjects; this result is consistent with the PFS result.

The subsequent treatments after PD in both arms were not balanced. Compared with the patients in senaparib group, more patients treated by placebo received active agents such as platinum, VEGF/VEGFR inhibitors and PARP inhibitors etc. However, favourable OS trend still continued to be observed in senaparib group supporting the initial data. The Applicant also conducted Post-hoc IPCW and censoring analyses, their results also seem to support this observed trend.

Median PFS2 according to investigator was not reached in both treatment arms although limitation have been highlighted above regarding the analysis of PFS2. Results from the TOI of FACT-O do not suggest a difference between placebo and senaparib group, both evolving towards an improvement.

Overall, although OS data are still immature, the provided analyses do not suggest a detrimental effect at the 2 nd interim analysis. Apart from PFS and OS, other secondary endpoints were not included in the multiplicity adjustment procedure, and should therefore be considered exploratory, nevertheless, these either support the benefit of senaparib compared to placebo, or do not suggest a detrimental effect.

## Subgroup analyses

Analyses of PFS according to BICR or investigator for all predefined subgroups (including FIGO stage, response to first line therapy, ECOG, age, neoadjuvant therapy) were consistent with the primary PFS analysis, including subgroup analyses by baseline BRCA status, best response to chemotherapy, prior neoadjuvant therapy, HRD analysis, with however larger 95% CI as sample sizes were very limited.

In particular, analysis of PFS by BICR according to BRCA status (mutated/wildtype) were similar and consistent with the primary analysis, with a HR of 0.43 (95% CI: 0.24, 0.76) in BRCAm patients and 0.43 (95% CI: 0.30, 0.61) in BRCAwt patients, and P values of 0.0026 and &lt;0.0001 respectively. Analysis of PFS by investigators were consistent with these results. In this pre-planned subgroup analysis, BRCAwt patients include HRD+/BRCAwt patients and HRP patients.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

At the cut off (17 May 2024) median OS in senaparib and placebo arms in patients with BRCA mutations were not reached. In BRCA wild type patients, median OS was not reached in senaparib group and was 45.57 months (40.411, NA) in placebo arm.

The relationship between HRD and efficacy was added as an exploratory objective during the course of the study. On the tumour tissue samples collected from 271 patients, HRD results test data from 222 patients were available (due mainly to poor DNA quality or substandard sequencing data quality), representing approximately 55% of the study population, limiting thus the generalizability. Among these 222 patients, median PFS was not reached in senaparib group and was 16.6 months (95% CI 11.6 - 21.4) in the placebo group, with a hazard ratio (HR) of 0.46 (95% CI 0.30 - 0.70). Nevertheless, when described by HRD and BRCA status, results from HRD positive (n=161), and HRDpositive/BRCAwt patients (n=86) and undetermined HRD status (n=182) were overall consistent with the primary analysis suggesting a benefit of senaparib over placebo on mPFS.

In HRP (HRD negative) population the median PFS in the senaparib and placebo arms were 27.1 months (95% CI 11.1-NR), and 19.5 months (95% CI 7.5-NR) respectively, with an HR of 0.66 (95% CI 0.34-1.29). The confidence interval remains wide and does not allow for a reliable estimation of the treatment effect. Although the applicant is seeking an indication in an all-comer population, a demonstration of efficacy specifically in HRP patients is not feasible as the study was not designed to assess efficacy in this subgroup. These results seem however consistent with findings from other PARP inhibitors, indicating that the magnitude of benefit in this population is markedly attenuated compared to HRD-positive or overall populations. This is also consistent with a biological plausibility of a lower activity in HRP.

Based on the study design and data provided, the indication cannot be restricted by biomarker status (e.g. to HRD-positive patients).

The applicant provided the OS results in subgroups by BRCA mutation and by HRD status (at the time of the first interim analysis). Some degree of uncertainty on the magnitude of effect remains for patients defined as biomarker-negative based on results of tests for BRCA1/2 mutations or HR deficiency due to these missing subgroup analyses results. The final OS analysis results of the pivotal study that include subgroups based on BRCA1/2 mutations and HRD biomarkers will be submitted as a PAES.

## Supportive studies

Two additional supportive studies (on top of the dose escalation study IMP4297-2016-CN01) were provided by the applicant: IMP4297-2018-CN01 (study 201), was a single arm phase II study in patients with advanced ovarian cancer carrying germline and/or somatic BRCA1/2 mutations. ORR assessed by IRC was 66.3% (61/92, 95% CI: 55.70%-75.83%). IMP4297-2016-AU01 (AU01), was a dose escalation phase I study in subjects with advanced solid tumours. PR (no CR) were observed in 13.6% of patients (one response at each dose level of 20 mg, 100mg and 120 mg). The overall Disease Control Rate (DCR) was 81.8% (18/22). Among the 8 subjects carrying BRCA mutations, the DCR was 83.3% (5/8, 95% CI: 35.9, 99.6)

Overall, study AU01 was a dose escalation study, with very preliminary results, which cannot be considered supportive, and Study 201 was conducted in another setting, with different objectives.

Therefore, these results cannot be considered as supportive in the claimed indication. At most, these results suggested an activity of senaparib in patients with ovarian cancer.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.3.8.2. Conclusions on the clinical efficacy

The results of the pivotal study show a PFS improvement with senaparib monotherapy compared to placebo for the maintenance treatment of adult patients with advanced epithelial (FIGO Stages III and IV) high-grade ovarian, fallopian tube or primary peritoneal cancer who are in response (complete or partial) after first-line platinum-based chemotherapy. Overall, the magnitude of the observed PFS benefit of senaparib compared to placebo in the maintenance treatment of patients who achieved PR or CR after a first line of platinum therapy can be considered clinically relevant and statistically significant. The magnitude of OS benefit, however, remains uncertain due to the data immaturity but data do not suggest a detrimental effect.

The main outcomes from a number of sensitivity analyses show the consistency with the primary analysis on PFS. This includes mainly BRCAm patients. An all-comers indication is accepted, considering the data available in subgroups by HRD status for senaparib and for other PARPi in the same setting. The extent of the benefit in the HRP population remains uncertain.

In order to further characterise the efficacy of senaparib, the applicant will submit the final overall survival results (including by subgroups based on BRCA1/2 mutations and HRD biomarkers) of study IMP4297-301 FLAMES as a PAES in Annex II.D by 30 June 2027 (see section 1.10.6).

## 5.4. Clinical safety

Please refer to the table of studies in section 5.1.2.

For the purpose of this document, the following definitions apply:

'Adverse event - AE' means any untoward medical occurrence in a subject to whom a medicinal product is administered and which does not necessarily have a causal relationship with this treatment.

'Serious adverse event - SAE' means any untoward medical occurrence that at any dose requires inpatient hospitalisation or prolongation of existing hospitalisation, results in persistent or significant disability or incapacity, results in a congenital anomaly or birth defect, is life-threatening, or results in death. The definition (in line with ICH E2A) includes important medical events that may not be immediately life-threatening or result in death or hospitalisation but may jeopardise the patient or may require intervention to prevent one of the other outcomes listed in the definition above.

'Adverse drug reaction - ADR' means any untoward and unintended response to a medicinal product related to any dose administered, for which, after a thorough assessment, a causal relationship between the medicinal product and the adverse event is at least a reasonable possibility, based for example, on their comparative incidence in clinical trials, or findings from epidemiological studies and/or on an evaluation of causality from individual case reports.

All tumour patients were predominantly at tumour stages III or IV. Among them, in Study 301, the majority of tumour patients in the senaparib group had disease of Stage III (70.3%); Study 201 was mainly Stage III (59.1%); in Studies 2016-CN01 and AU01, Stage IV was predominant (accounting for 93% and 90.9%, respectively).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The BRCA mutation status of tumour patients was as follows: all tumour patients in Study 201, 56.1% of patients in Study 2016-CN01, 34.7% of patients in the senaparib group of Study 301, and 23.5% of patients in Study AU01, were BRCA mutant.

The lines of therapy experienced by tumour patients were as follows: in the senaparib group of Study 301, all tumour patients had received first-line platinum-based chemotherapy; all patients in Study 201 had received ≥2 lines of therapy; in Studies CN01 and AU01, the majority of patients had previously received ≥3 lines of therapy (accounting for 61.4% and 46.9%, respectively).

## 5.4.3.3. Adverse drug reactions

Safety data for the tumour patients who received senaparib in clinical studies (studies 2016-CN01, AU01, 201, and 301) served as the source of adverse reaction evaluation (N=459).

All AEs meeting the below criteria were considered as potential ADRs:

- TEAEs from the pivotal study IMP4297-301 with incidence greater than 2% in senaparib group vs. placebo group.
- Grade ≥3 TEAEs with frequency ≥ 0.5% in all tumour patients across the four studies.
- SAE with frequency ≥ 0.5% in all tumour patients across the four studies.

All potential ADRs were medically assessed, taking into consideration the below factors:

- Severity and outcome
- Time of event onset
- Mode-of-action of the drug
- Known PARP-inhibitor class effect
- Confounding factor of the underlying disease
- De-challenge/rechallenge result

All ADRs are presented in Table 46. The ADR table is based on 447 patients with tumours who received senaparib at doses of ≤100 mg/day. The total safety population comprised 459 patients (see Table 45); however, 12 patients from two Phase I studies who received doses &gt;100 mg/day were excluded from the dataset used to derive the ADR table.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Adverse drug reactions are listed by MedDRA System Organ Class (SOC) and then by MedDRA preferred term. Within each SOC, preferred terms are arranged by decreasing frequency. Frequencies of occurrence of adverse reactions are defined as: very common (≥ 1/10); common (≥ 1/100 to &lt; 1/10); uncommon (≥ 1/1 000 to &lt; 1/100); rare (≥ 1/10 000 to &lt; 1/1 000); very rare (&lt; 1/10 000).

Subjects experiencing multiple adverse events within the same system organ classification are counted only once for that system organ classification. Similarly, subjects experiencing multiple adverse events within the same preferred term are counted only once within that preferred term.

Between the two cutoff dates, there was no change to SAEs occurred during TEAE reporting period because the majority of patients (401/403) had ended study treatment by 17 May 2024. However, beyond the TEAE reporting period, three subjects in the senaparib group reported MDS/AML, and one previously reported MDS was aggregated into acute leukaemia. However, the total number of subjects experiencing MDS/AML has remained unchanged since 31-May-2025.

## 5.4.5. Adverse Events leading to dose reduction, treatment interruption or permanent discontinuation

## 5.4.5.1. Study 301

## Adverse Events Leading to Dose Reduction

TEAEs leading to dose reduction with incidence ≥ 1% by PT in senaparib group or the placebo group included (in descending order of the incidence for senaparib group; included in the parentheses are incidences for senaparib group and the placebo group, respectively): Anaemia (82 [30.4%] vs. 0); Platelet count decreased (78 [28.9%] vs. 0); Neutrophil count decreased (50 [18.5%] vs. 3 [2.3%]); White blood cell count decreased (41 [15.2%] vs. 2 [1.5%]).

## Adverse Events Leading to Treatment Interruption

TEAEs leading to dose interruption with incidence ≥5% in senaparib group compared to placebo group include platelet count decreased (41.5% vs. 0), anaemia (39.3% vs. 0), neutrophil count decreased (34.4% vs. 5.3%), and white blood cell count decreased (29.6% vs. 2.3%).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Adverse Events Leading To Permanent Discontinuation

Fourteen subjects (5.2%) in senaparib group experienced TEAEs leading to permanent discontinuation, and 13/270 subjects (4.8%) experienced TRAEs leading to permanent discontinuation.

TEAEs leading to permanent discontinuation by PT in senaparib group included: platelet count decreased (8 [3.0%]), anaemia (5 [1.9%]), and alanine aminotransferase increased (1 [0.4%]), and vitreous floater (1 [0.4%]).

## 5.4.5.2. Pooled analysis

## Adverse Events Leading to Dose Reduction

Among 54 tumour patients receiving &lt;100 mg senaparib, no subjects experienced TEAE leading to dose reduction.

Among 405 tumour patients receiving ≥100 mg senaparib, 218 (53.8%) experienced at least one TEAE leading to dose reduction, all related to the study drug. By SOC classification, TEAE with incidence ≥5% included blood and lymphatic system disorders (50.9%). By PT classification, TEAEs with an incidence of ≥5% included: Anaemia (27.4%); Thrombocytopenia (22.2%); Neutropenia (14.3%); Leukopenia (12.3%).

Among 459 tumour patients receiving senaparib, 218 (47.5%) experienced at least one TEAE leading to dose reduction, all related to the study drug. By SOC classification, TEAE with incidence ≥5% included blood and lymphatic system disorders (44.9%). By PT classification, TEAEs with an incidence of ≥5% included: Anaemia (24.2%); Thrombocytopenia (19.6%); Neutropenia (12.6%); Leukopenia (10.9%).

Among 447 tumour patients receiving senaparib at ≤100 mg/day, the common adverse reactions that led to dose reduction included anaemia (24.8%), thrombocytopenia (20.1%), neutropenia (13.0%), and leukopenia (11.2%).

## Adverse Events Leading to Treatment Interruption

Among 54 tumour patients receiving &lt;100 mg senaparib, 10 (18.5%) experienced at least one TEAE leading to dose interruption. By SOC classification, TEAEs with incidence ≥5% included blood and lymphatic system disorders (5.6%). By PT classification, no events had incidence exceeding 5%, with anaemia being the most common PT (3.7%). All TEAEs under the blood and lymphatic system disorders SOC were drug-related (3 subjects, 5.6%), while other TEAEs were not related to the study drug.

Among 405 tumour patients receiving ≥100 mg senaparib, 280 (69.1%) experienced at least one TEAE leading to dose interruption. By SOC classification, TEAEs leading to dose interruption with incidence ≥ 5% included (in descending order of incidence): Blood and lymphatic system disorders (61.2%); Infections and infestations (8.6%); Gastrointestinal disorders (6.9%). By PT classification, TEAEs leading to dose interruption with incidence ≥5% included (listed in descending order of incidence): Anaemia (37.5%); Thrombocytopenia (33.3%); Neutropenia (27.4%); Leukopenia (24.9%); COVID-19 (5.9%).

Among 459 tumour patients receiving senaparib, 290 (63.2%) experienced at least one TEAE leading to dose interruption. By SOC classification, TEAEs with incidence ≥5% included (in descending order of incidence): Blood and lymphatic system disorders (54.7%); Infections and infestations (8.1%); Gastrointestinal disorders (6.5%). By PT classification, TEAEs leading to dose interruption with incidence ≥5% included (listed in descending order of incidence): Anaemia (33.6%); Thrombocytopenia (29.6%); Neutropenia (24.4%); Leukopenia (22.2%); COVID-19 (5.2%).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Among 447 tumour patients receiving senaparib at ≤100 mg/day, the common adverse reactions that led to dose interruption included anaemia (34.2%), thrombocytopenia (30.4%), neutropenia (25.1%), leukopenia (22.8%), intestinal obstruction (2.0%), vomiting (2.0%), lymphopenia (1.8%), transaminases increased (1.8%), pyrexia (1.8%), nausea (1.6%), abdominal pain (1.6%), and diarrhoea (1.3%).

## Adverse events leading to permanent discontinuation

Among 54 tumour patients receiving &lt;100 mg senaparib, 7 subjects (13.0%) experienced at least one TEAE leading to permanent discontinuation. By SOC classification, TEAEs leading to permanent discontinuation with incidence ≥3% included blood and lymphatic system disorders (5.6%, all related to the investigational drug) and gastrointestinal disorders (3.7%). By PT classification, TEAEs leading to permanent discontinuation with an incidence ≥3% included thrombocytopenia and anaemia (both 3.7%, all related to the investigational drug). Three subjects (5.6%) experienced at least one TRAE leading to permanent discontinuation. By SOC classification, TRAEs leading to permanent discontinuation with an incidence ≥3% included blood and lymphatic system disorders (5.6%).

Among 405 patients with tumours who received ≥100 mg senaparib, 26 (6.4%) experienced at least one TEAE leading to permanent discontinuation. By SOC classification, TEAEs leading to permanent discontinuation with an incidence ≥3% included blood and lymphatic system disorders (4.2%, all study drug-related). By PT classification, no events had incidence exceeding 3%. In 21 (5.2%) subjects, at least one TRAE leading to permanent discontinuation occurred. By SOC classification, study TRAEs leading to permanent discontinuation with an incidence of ≥3% included blood and lymphatic system disorders (4.2%). By PT classification, no events had incidence exceeding 3%.

Among 459 tumour patients receiving senaparib, 33 (7.2%) experienced at least one TEAE leading to permanent discontinuation. By SOC classification, TEAEs leading to permanent discontinuation with an incidence ≥3% included blood and lymphatic system disorders (4.4%, all study drug-related). By PT classification, no events had incidence exceeding 3%. A total of 24 (5.2%) subjects experienced at least one TRAE leading to permanent discontinuation. By SOC classification, TRAEs leading to permanent discontinuation with an incidence ≥3% included blood and lymphatic system disorders (4.4%). By PT classification, no events had incidence exceeding 3%.

Among 447 tumour patients receiving senaparib at ≤100 mg/day, the common adverse reactions leading to permanent discontinuation included thrombocytopenia (2.9%) and anaemia (2.0%).

Haematologic toxicity

In tumour patients treated with ≤100 mg/day of senaparib, the overall incidence of haematologic toxicity was 83.4%, with ≥Grade 3 events occurring in 51.7% of patients. One fatal case of bone marrow failure was reported in clinical studies.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The incidence of anaemia was 71.8%, and the incidence of ≥Grade 3 anaemia was 30.2%. The median time from the first dose to the first onset was 32 days (range: 7 to 673 days), and the median duration was 31 days (range: 3 to 648 days). The incidence of drug interruption, dose reduction and permanent discontinuation due to anaemia was 34.2%, 24.8% and 2.0%, respectively.

The incidence of leukopenia was 62.6%, and the incidence of ≥Grade 3 leukopenia was 12.3%. The median time from the first dose to the first onset was 29 days (range: 2 to 723 days), and the median duration was 17 days (range: 2 to 400 days). The incidence of drug interruption, dose reduction and permanent discontinuation due to leukopenia was 22.8%, 11.2%, and 0.2%, respectively.

The incidence of neutropenia was 59.7%, and the incidence of ≥Grade 3 neutropenia was 20.1%. The median time from the first dose to the first onset was 30 days (range: 2 to 723 days), and the median duration was 15 days (range: 2 to 391 days). The incidence of drug interruption, dose reduction and permanent discontinuation due to neutropenia was 25.1%, 13.0% and 0.2%, respectively.

The incidence of thrombocytopenia was 58.2%, and the incidence of ≥Grade 3 thrombocytopenia was 20.8%. The median time from the first dose to the first onset was 28 days (range: 7 to 784 days), and the median duration was 22 days (range: 2.5 to 361 days). The incidence of drug interruption, dose reduction and permanent discontinuation due to thrombocytopenia was 30.4%, 20.1% and 2.9%.

The incidence of lymphopenia was 13.0%, and the incidence of ≥Grade 3 lymphopenia was 2.2%. The median time from the first dose to the first onset was 56.5 days (range: 2 to 674 days), and the median duration was 13.75 days (range: 2 to 84 days). The incidence of drug interruption and dose reduction due to lymphopenia was 1.8% and 0.2%, and no patient permanently discontinued treatment.

## Gastrointestinal toxicities

Nausea and vomiting are frequently reported with senaparib, with the incidence of 26.8% and 15.2% respectively. However, most of them were Grade 1 or 2. Incidence of ≥Grade 3 nausea and vomiting were both 0.7%.

The median time from the first dose to the first onset of nausea was 6 days (range: 1 to 603 days), and the median duration was 18 days (range: 1 to 657 days). The incidence of drug interruption and dose reduction due to nausea was 1.6% and 0.4%, respectively. No patient permanently discontinued treatment.

The median time from the first dose to the first onset of vomiting was 19.5 days (range: 1 to 721 days), and the median duration was 1 day (range: 1 to 1151 days). The incidence of drug interruption, dose reduction and permanent discontinuation due to vomiting was 2.0%, 0.4% and 0.2%, respectively.

The incidence of diarrhoea was 13.6%, and the incidence of ≥Grade 3 diarrhoea was 0.7%. The median time from the first dose to the first onset was 45 days (range: 1 to 689 days), and the median duration was 3 days (range: 1 to 387 days). The incidence of drug interruption and dose reduction due to diarrhoea was 1.3% and 0.2%, respectively. No patient permanently discontinued treatment.

The incidence of intestinal obstruction was 4.0%, and the incidence of ≥Grade 3 was 1.8%, with a fatal outcome in 1 patient (0.2%). The underlying disease may play a role in the development of intestinal obstruction in patients with ovarian cancer. The median time from the first dose to the first onset was 63 days (range: 2 to 664 days), and the median duration was 9 days (range: 2 to 106 days). The incidence of drug interruption, dose reduction and permanent discontinuation due to intestinal obstruction was 2.0%, 0.4% and 0.2%, respectively.

## Myelodysplastic syndrome / acute myeloid leukaemia

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

During the period of 17-May-2024 and 31-Dec-2025, 3 subjects in the senaparib group of study 301 reported MDS/AML cases, and one previously reported MDS was aggregated into acute leukaemia. All of these occurred during long-term survival follow up. With a median follow-up duration of 51.17 months, 6/270 patients in senaparib group reported MDS / AML in the 301 study. No case was reported in placebo group. In total, five out of the six patients died.

A total of 7 patients in clinical studies developed MDS / AML, representing an overall incidence of 1.6%, including events reported during the long-term survival follow up. The median duration of therapy prior to developing MDS / AML was 729 days (range: 392 to 1007 days). The median time from the first dose to the first onset of MDS / AML was 1035 days (range: 393 to 1514 days). All patients had potential contributing factors for the development of MDS / AML, such as prior chemotherapy with platinum agents. Some also received platinum and paclitaxel during the period of senaparib discontinuation and the onset of MDS / AML.

## 5.4.7.4. Use in pregnancy and lactation

No pregnancy events were reported during the studies, which is to be expected as pregnant subjects were excluded. Male subjects were advised to use contraception for the duration specified in the study protocol.

## 5.4.7.5. Overdose

The RP2D for senaparib is set at 100 mg. In the dose-escalation study, some subjects received single dose of senaparib exceeding 100 mg QD, with the highest dose being 150 mg QD. Compared to subjects who received a single dose of senaparib 100 mg QD, there were no significant differences in safety profiles.

In the Phase II 201 study, 3 subjects each reported 1 event of accidental overdose, with the highest single dose being 160 mg. These overdose events did not result in AEs that were different from those at the 100 mg dose.

In the pivotal Phase III 301 study, 6 subjects each reported one event of accidental overdose, with the highest dose being 160 mg daily. These overdose events did not result in AEs that were different from those at the 100 mg dose.

## 5.4.8. Immunological events

Not applicable.

## 5.4.9. Safety related to drug-drug interactions and other interactions

No dedicated safety DDI study was conducted. Given senaparib QT prolonging effect, caution of use is advised in SmPC section 4.5 in case senaparib association with other QT prolonging drugs could not be avoided. The risk is increased of serious adverse event caused by potentiation of QT prolonging effect.

## 5.4.11. Post-marketing experience

On 14 January 2025, senaparib was approved in China. Up to 31 May 2025, about 170 patients had been exposed to marketed senaparib. One post-marketing safety report of 'myelosuppression' was received.

## 5.4.12. In vitro biomarker test for patient selection for safety

Not applicable.

## 5.4.13. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical safety

## 5.4.13.1. Discussion

## 5.4.13.1.1. Overall assessment of available safety data

The data for the safety evaluation of senaparib have been collected in 6 clinical studies:

- The pivotal study (IMP4297- 301), in which, patients with FIGO stage III-IV ovarian cancer who already received first-line platinum-based chemotherapy, received the claimed dose of 100mg of senaparib, QD, as monotherapy maintenance. The applicant also provided updated safety data with a more recent cut-off date of 31 Dec 2025.
- Three supportive studies, including one conducted in patients with advanced ovarian cancer subjects with germline and/or somatic BRCA1/2 mutations and who received the recommended dosage schedule (IMP4297-2018-CN01); and 2 studies conducted in patients with advanced solid tumours who received different doses of senaparib, ranging from 2mg to 150mg QD or 50mg BID (IMP4297-2016-CN01 and IMP4297-2016-AU01). Those 3 studies as well as the pivotal study constituted the pooled safety analysis.
- Two additional studies were conducted, one to investigate drug interactions in healthy subjects (IMP4297- 104) and the other to analyse intrinsic factors such as race in patients with advanced solid tumours (IMP4297- 106).

Exposure, demographics and baseline characteristics

The main safety analysis was based on data from the pivotal study IMP4297-301 that included 403 subjects, with 270 in the senaparib group and 133 in the placebo group.

Regarding demographic and baseline characteristics, patients in both groups were comparable, in terms of age, race, type of tumour, proportion of BRCA mutated and proportion of complete response to the first line of chemotherapy.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

As of the updated cut-off date of 31 Dec 2025 for the pivotal study 301, the median exposure duration slightly increased from 698.5 to 701.5 days in senaparib group (N=270) vs 348.0 days in placebo group (N=133). However, the median dose intensity in the senaparib group was lower than the claimed posology (77.445 vs 100mg/day). This is explained by the important number of patients who had a dose reduction (174 [64.4%]); among them, 173 had a reduction to the 80mg dose, 106 to the 60mg dose and 50 to the 40mg dose. As of March 16, 2023, in the senaparib group, 36 patients were treated for &gt;6 months to &lt;12 months and 176 patients for more than 12 months. Among the 176 patients, 70 received a dosage between 80-100mg, 51 a dosage between 60-80mg and 55 a dosage &lt;60mg. Thus, comparable safety data to establish the long-term safety profile at the claimed initial posology of 100 mg daily were available for 95 participants.

The supportive pooled analysis included 459 patients, divided according to dose: 405 received ≥100 mg senaparib (from studies IMP4297-2016-CN01, IMP4297-2016-AU01, IMP4297-2018-CN01 and IMP4297- 301) and 54 received &lt;100 mg senaparib (from studies IMP4297-2016-CN01 and IMP4297-2016-AU01).

Studies included in the supportive pooled analysis had different designs and indications (including breast cancer, prostate cancer and lung cancer), so the demographic and baseline characteristics between dose groups were not comparable: the proportion of female participants was higher in the ≥100 mg senaparib group than in &lt;100 mg senaparib group (395 [97.5%] vs 38 [70.4%]). In terms of race, 391 [96.5%] participants were Asian in ≥100 mg senaparib group while the population in &lt;100 mg senaparib group was composed by 31 [57.4%] Asian and 22 [40.7%] White participants. The same applies for the tumour type, 381 [94.1%] patients had ovarian cancer in the first group vs 23 [42.6%] in the second. BRCA mutation status, tumour stage, and line of therapy varied systematically across the contributing studies. Accordingly, the pooled analysis is considered exploratory.

Potential ADRs supporting SmPC section 4.8, were selected based on the TEAEs from the pivotal study IMP4297-301 with incidence greater than 2% in senaparib group vs. placebo group, grade ≥3 TEAEs with frequency ≥ 0.5% in all tumour patients (treated with 100mg senaparib) across the four studies and SAEs with frequency ≥ 0.5% in all tumour patients (treated with 100mg senaparib) across the four studies.

The median exposure duration was four times longer in ≥100 mg senaparib group than in &lt;100 mg senaparib group (12.1 vs 2.8 months), and overall, 207 participants received senaparib for &gt;12 months, including 88 patients were at a dosage between 80-100mg. Since the analysis is based on dose groups, patients are not exposed to the same dose. The median dose intensity in the ≥100 mg senaparib group is lower than the claimed dose (80.5 vs 100 mg/day) as in the senaparib group in the pivotal study.

In summary, exposure and patient characteristics differed between the two groups in the pooled analysis, however, these differences were expected and did not raise any major concern.

## Adverse events

In the pivotal study, almost all patients (269 [99.6%]) had at least 1 TEAEs in the senaparib group and most of which were related (267 [98.9%]). Overall, the senaparib group had higher incidence of TEAE (269 [99.6%] vs 131 [98.5%]), TRAEs (267 [98.9%] vs 109 [82.0%]), Grade ≥3 TEAEs (182 [67.4%] vs 27 [20.3%]), SAEs (77 [28.5%] vs 5 [3.8%]), TEAEs leading to treatment interruption (207 [76.7%] vs 26 [19.5%]), TEAEs leading to dose reduction (172 [63.7%] vs 8 [6.0%]) and TEAEs leading to discontinuation (15 [5.6%] vs 0), compared to placebo group.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The most common TEAEs in senaparib group were haematologic events (belong to the SOCs Investigations or Blood and lymphatic system disorders) including anaemia (219 [81.9%] vs 25 [18.8%]), neutrophil count decreased (206 [76.3%] vs 42 [31.6%]), white blood cell count decreased (203 [75.2%] vs 38 [28.6%]), and platelet count decreased (189 [70.0%] vs 21 [15.8%]). Regarding other events, the most frequent TEAEs belonged to the SOCs Investigations (transaminases increased: 150 [55.6%] vs 56 [42.1%]), Gastrointestinal disorders (nausea: 76 [28.1%] vs 13 [9.8%]) and General disorders (asthenia: 58 [21.5%] vs 11 [8.3%]). The most common Grade ≥ 3 TEAEs were haematological events, including: anaemia, thrombocytopenia, neutropenia, and leukopenia.

The most frequent TEAEs overall in the supportive pooled analysis were consistent with the common TEAEs in the senaparib-treated patients of the pivotal study and belonged to the SOCs Blood and lymphatic system disorders (anaemia, leukopenia, neutropenia and thrombocytopenia), Gastrointestinal disorders (nausea) and Investigations (transaminases increased). The pattern of adverse events is comparable to that observed for other PARP inhibitors.

For the assessment of ADRs, the TEAEs from the pivotal study with an incidence greater than 2% in the senaparib group compared to the placebo group were considered as well as TREAEs grade ≥ 3 and SAE with a frequency ≥ 0.5% in the supportive pooled analysis. These potential adverse events were then evaluated according to criteria considered appropriate.

## Serious adverse events and death

In the pivotal study, a higher incidence of SAE was observed in the senaparib group compared to the placebo group (77 [28.5%] vs 5 [3.8%]). Most were haematological events, belonging to the SOCs Blood and lymphatic system disorders or Investigations (anaemia (26 [9.6%]), platelet count decreased (21 [7.8%]) and Gastrointestinal disorders (intestinal obstruction (7 [2.6%]). The observations in the pooled analysis were in line with the pivotal study in terms of type and frequency of SAEs.

The incidence of death in the senaparib group was lower than that of the placebo group (61 [22.6%] vs 39 [29.3%]). No death occurred within 30 days after the last dose of study drug. The main cause of death was disease progression in both groups (41 [67.2%] vs. 25 [64.1%]). No deaths were considered related to an AE. In the senaparib group, 12 deaths (19.7%) had an unknown cause and 8 (13.1%) were attributed to other causes; several occurred long after treatment discontinuation.

In the supportive pooled analysis, 5 (1.1%) additional subjects experienced fatal TEAEs, included cerebral haemorrhage and cerebral infarction occurred in senaparib ≥100mg group; and intestinal obstruction, bone marrow failure, and haemorrhagic shock occurred in senaparib &lt;100mg group.

## Dose reduction, treatment interruption and discontinuation

In the pivotal study, the proportion of dose reduction and treatment interruption due to an AE in senaparib group was significantly higher than in placebo group (172 [63.7%] vs 8 [6.0%] and 207 [76.7%] vs 26 [19.5%] respectively). In both cases, the most frequent cause was the occurrence of haematological events including anaemia, platelet count decreased, neutrophil count decreased, white blood cell counts decreased, which is in accordance with the reported safety profile of senaparib. The results of the supportive pooled analysis were similar, with a higher proportion of dose reduction and treatment interruption in patients who received ≥100 mg senaparib compared to &lt;100 mg senaparib group. This is in favour of a dose-related toxicity of senaparib.

Regarding TEAE leading to permanent discontinuation in the pivotal study, 14 [5.2%] patients in the senaparib group experienced at least one TEAE leading to permanent discontinuation, including platelet count decreased, anaemia, alanine aminotransferase increased and vitreous floater. All were considered drug related except the vitreous floater.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The fact that 63.7% of participants reduced the initial dose and 76.7% interrupted senaparib raises uncertainty about establishing the safety profile of senaparib at the claimed dose of 100 mg. However, the overall low rate of discontinuation due to TEAEs shows that the toxicity of senaparib could be manageable with dose reductions and interruptions as recommended in section 4.2 of the SmPC.

## Adverse Events of Special Interest (AESI)

The AESIs considered by the applicant for senaparib were haematological toxicity, MDS/AML, noninfectious pneumonitis and second primary malignancy.

As described above, significant proportion of haematological toxicity was observed, including anaemia, neutropenia, leukopenia, and thrombocytopenia. Among the 459 tumour patients who received senaparib and experienced hematologic toxicity events, the median time to onset was 17 days (2-784 days), and the median duration was 22.5 days (2.5-648 days). These events led to dose reduction in 44.7% of subjects, dose interruption in 54.7%, and permanent discontinuation in 4.4%. The majority of these events were resolved and few cases led to permanent discontinuation. The haematological events are listed in section 4.8 of the SmPC as very common. Testing complete blood count before treatment with senaparib is recommended. Senaparib should not be started until patients have recovered from haematological toxicity caused by prior anti-tumour therapy (absolute neutrophil count should recover to ≥1.5 x 109/L, platelet count should recover to ≥75 x 109/L, and haemoglobin should recover to ≥10 g/dL). Testing complete blood counts is recommended biweekly for the first three months, followed by monthly monitoring for the next 3 months of treatment, and periodically after this time, to monitor for clinically significant changes in any haematologic parameter during treatment. Patients with low body weight/BMI, low baseline platelet values and hypoalbuminemia may be at increased risk of haematological toxicity and closely monitoring is required (see sections 4.2 and 4.4 of the SmPC).

In addition, section 4.2 of the SmPC provides recommended dose modifications for ADRs related to haematological toxicity. Hence, routine pharmacovigilance and routine risk minimisation measures are considered sufficient to address the haematological toxicity of senaparib.

Three cases of MDS/AML occurred among the 459 tumour patients who received senaparib, all related to senaparib: 2 AML events occurred before the 30-day safety follow up and were considered as TEAEs; one MDS occurred during long-term survival follow up. In all cases, patients experienced platelet count decreased that led to permanent discontinuation of treatment prior to their diagnosis. The median time to onset was 714 days (393- 1035 days). Patients were treated for their AML/MDS, but at the cut-off date, these events had not yet resolved. After the cut-off date, 4 additional MDS/AML cases were reported. Overall, up to 31 May 2025, a total of 7 subjects (1.6%) developed MDS/AML among 447 tumour patients who received ≤100 mg senaparib developed MDS/AML while no cases are observed in the placebo group. Among these 7 events, one patient died due to respiratory failure. MDS/AML are currently listed in section 4.8 of the SmPC as common adverse reactions. If haematologic toxicity does not recover to ≤CTCAE Grade 1 for a long time (&gt;4 weeks), or MDS / AML is suspected, the patient should be further consulted by a haematologist. If MDS / AML is confirmed during the treatment, Sepalna must be discontinued immediately, and appropriate treatment shall be given (see section 4.4 of the SmPC). These events will be included in the safety concerns in future PSURs to allow further characterisation with regard to frequency, time to onset, and outcome.

Second Primary Malignancy (other than MDS/AML): Two acute myeloid leukaemia and one metastasis to bone were found. However, these events were not considered as second primary malignancies (other than MDS/AML). No events occurred in this category and consequently, this AESI was not listed in the SmPC. However, this safety topic will be closely monitored in the future PSURs, based on a comprehensive and detailed collection of data to allow an appropriate clinical evaluation with causality assessment.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Non-infectious Pneumonitis: among 459 tumour patients who received senaparib, 5 (1.1%) subjects experienced pneumonitis, including 1 related Grade 1 and 1 non-related Grade ≥3 pneumonitis in a subject who interrupted senaparib. Based on these cases, it was not possible to conclude on a causal relationship with senaparib so no further action is required.

## Laboratory and other finding

Regarding haematological tests, the abnormal test results were consistent with the haematological toxicity of senaparib reported in the section on AEs.

Regarding biochemistry tests, among the highlighted parameters only transaminase increased (ALT/AST) was listed in the ADR table. Regarding blood alkaline phosphatase increased, hypercalcaemia and hypoalbuminemia, laboratory tests show an incidence of more than 20% in senaparib group with a difference of at least 5% with placebo group. In addition, hypomagnesaemia was reported in more than 40% of patients in the senaparib group, 6.4% higher than placebo group.

A potential effect of senaparib concerning liver toxicity has been shown in non-clinical data, despite additional information regarding the mechanism of action is requested. There were no cases of Hy's law observed. The applicant indicated that no cases of DILI have been reported in the pivotal study and in study IMP4297-2018-CN01. Higher incidences of bilirubinaemia (11.5% vs 2.3%), increased GGT levels (14.1% vs 5.3%), elevated transaminases (32.2% vs 25.6%), and blood alkaline phosphatase increased (8.1% vs 3.0%) were observed. This is adequately reflected in section 4.8 of the SmPC.

The pooled analysis identified cases of QTc prolongation, including cases associated with clinical ventricular arrhythmia. Among 393 tumour patients treated with senaparib 100mg QD 9,4% experienced QTc prolongation or QTc prolongations considered clinically concerning. Although the available findings highlight the need for a rigorous and comprehensive non-clinical evaluation of senaparib' s potential for QT prolongation, such an evaluation remains lacking and has not been pursued further. Senaparib should be used with caution in patients at risk of QT prolongation and arrhythmias, including patients with electrolyte disturbances, genetic predisposition, co-prescription of QT-prolonging medicinal products, or intermittent clinical conditions predisposing to hypokalaemia. Periodic monitoring of electrocardiograms and serum electrolytes (potassium, magnesium) should be considered during treatment in patients at risk (see section 4.4 of the SmPC).

## Safety in special population or situation

Regarding age, no meaningful differences in the incidence of TEAE were observed between age groups in the senaparib group or in the pooled supportive studies. The proposed product information which indicates that no dose adjustment is required in elderly patients, is considered adequate.

Regarding BRCA status, patients with a BRCA mutation in the senaparib group had a higher incidence of senaparib-related TEAEs leading to dose reduction than BRCA wild-type disease. No other meaningful differences were observed.

Regarding race, clinical safety data from Asian and White patients with advanced solid tumours enrolled in studies IMP4297-2016-AU01, IMP4297-2016-CN01 and IM4297-106 were compared. Overall, the TEAEs reported were consistent with those observed in the pivotal and supportive studies. Differences in TEAE frequencies were observed, which may be attributable to the limited number of White patients in these studies. Nevertheless, the frequencies of adverse reactions reported in the product information are considered adequate.

The following warning has been included in the section 4.4. of the SmPC, with the reference to sections 4.5 and 4.6: Senaparib may cause embryo and foetal harm when administered to a pregnant woman based on its mechanism of action (PARP inhibition). It should not be used during pregnancy or in women of childbearing potential who are not using effective contraception. Women of childbearing potential are required to have a pregnancy test before treatment to rule out pregnancy. Given uncertainties whether senaparib may reduce the effectiveness of hormonal contraceptives, women using systemically acting hormonal contraceptives should add a barrier method during treatment and for at least 6 months after the last dose of senaparib.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Regarding laboratory tests, the results were consistent with the clinical observations in the studies CN01, AU01, 106 for the haematological parameters. However, regarding biochemistry, the incidence of these following parameters was higher in White people than Asian people: hypoalbuminaemia and blood alkaline phosphatase.

Nine patients experienced an accidental overdose with the highest dose being 160 mg daily. Furthermore, some subjects received a dose greater than 100 mg during dose-escalation studies with the highest dose being 150 mg daily. In both cases, this did not induce AEs different from those already known for the recommended dose of 100mg QD. The information included in the SmPC is therefore appropriate.

## Post-marketing experience

Among the 170 patients exposed in China since the MA of senaparib, 1 post-marketing safety report of 'myelosuppression' has been received.

## 5.4.13.1.2. Adverse drug reactions (ADRs) in the SmPC

The ADRs for inclusion in the SmPC are described in section 5.4.3.3. above.

## 5.4.13.2. Conclusions on clinical safety

Overall, the safety profile of senaparib is consistent with that of the PARP inhibitor class and is characterised mainly by haematological toxicity, which frequently requires dose interruption or reduction but rarely leads to permanent discontinuation. The identified risks, including MDS/AML, QT prolongation and potential drug-drug interactions, are adequately addressed in the product information and through routine pharmacovigilance and risk-minimisation measures. Despite uncertainties regarding long-term exposure at the proposed 100 mg dose, the available safety data are considered sufficient and the toxicity of senaparib is considered manageable.

## 6. Risk management plan

## 6.1. Safety specification

## 6.1.1. Proposed safety specification

The applicant proposed the following summary of safety concerns in the RMP v1.0 dated 31 July 2026:
